[0] COMPLETE HMO MASTER BLUEPRINT
[1] Enterprise HMO Management Platform
[2] Document Classification: Enterprise Master Blueprint
Industry: Health Maintenance Organization (HMO)
Primary Market: Nigeria
Platform Type: Enterprise Multi-Channel Platform
Architecture: API-first, modular, event-driven capable
Authorization: RBAC + ABAC + Scope-Based Access Control
Security Model: Zero Trust / Least Privilege
Currency: Nigerian Naira (₦), with extensibility for future currencies
Primary Pillars:
[3] Performance
[4] Security
[5] Scalability
[6] Reliability
[7] Usability
[9] PART I — PRODUCT & BUSINESS FOUNDATION
[10] Chapter 1 — Executive Summary
[11] 1.1 Product Definition
[12] The Enterprise HMO Management Platform is a centralized digital platform designed to manage the operational, healthcare, financial, administrative, compliance, and security activities of an HMO organization.
[13] The platform connects:
[14] HMO management
[15] HMO employees
[16] Members/enrollees
[17] Dependants
[18] Corporate employers
[19] Healthcare providers
[20] Hospitals
[21] Clinics
[22] Pharmacies
[23] Laboratories
[24] Finance teams
[25] Claims teams
[26] Medical teams
[27] Customer-service teams
[28] Compliance teams
[29] Auditors
[30] External systems
[31] through a controlled and auditable digital ecosystem.
[32] The system will provide different portals and interfaces while maintaining a centralized authorization and data-governance model.
[34] 1.2 Core Product Principle
[35] The platform must operate according to:
[36] No user receives access merely because they are an employee.
[37] Access must be explicitly granted through authorized policies.
[38] The authorization chain is:
[39] USER
[40] ↓
[41] IDENTITY
[42] ↓
[43] AUTHENTICATION
[44] ↓
[45] ORGANIZATION
[46] ↓
[47] DEPARTMENT
[48] ↓
[49] ROLE
[50] ↓
[51] PERMISSION
[52] ↓
[53] RESOURCE
[54] ↓
[55] DATA SCOPE
[56] ↓
[57] ATTRIBUTES
[58] ↓
[59] BUSINESS RULES
[60] ↓
[61] SEGREGATION OF DUTIES
[62] ↓
[63] APPROVAL REQUIREMENTS
[64] ↓
[65] AUTHORIZATION DECISION
[66] ↓
[67] AUDIT EVENT
[68] ↓
[69] SECURITY MONITORING
[70] The default authorization decision is:
[71] DENY
[72] Access is granted only when the system can establish that the user is authorized.
[74] Chapter 2 — Product Vision
[75] 2.1 Vision
[76] To build a secure, scalable and intelligent digital operating platform that enables an HMO to manage its entire healthcare ecosystem from one trusted system.
[77] The platform should eliminate fragmented processes such as:
[78] spreadsheets
[79] disconnected databases
[80] manual claim tracking
[81] uncontrolled document sharing
[82] manual approval chains
[83] duplicated member records
[84] disconnected provider records
[85] unstructured financial reconciliation
[86] uncontrolled employee access
[88] 2.2 Product Mission
[89] The mission is to provide:
[90] A single controlled platform where every HMO transaction, decision, user action and sensitive data access can be governed, monitored and audited.
[92] Chapter 3 — Business Objectives
[93] The platform shall pursue the following objectives.
[94] 3.1 Operational Efficiency
[95] Reduce manual processes across:
[96] enrollment
[97] claims
[98] authorization
[99] provider management
[100] billing
[101] payments
[102] customer service
[103] reporting
[105] 3.2 Security
[106] Protect:
[107] member information
[108] medical information
[109] financial information
[110] employer information
[111] provider information
[112] employee information
[113] authentication credentials
[114] system configuration
[116] 3.3 Transparency
[117] Every significant operation should be traceable.
[118] For example:
[119] Who performed the action?
[120] When?
[121] From which device?
[122] From which session?
[123] What resource was affected?
[124] What was the previous state?
[125] What changed?
[126] Why was it changed?
[127] Who approved it?
[128] What policy permitted it?
[130] 3.4 Automation
[131] Automate repetitive operations such as:
[132] eligibility verification
[133] renewal reminders
[134] provider credential expiry alerts
[135] claim validation
[136] duplicate detection
[137] approval routing
[138] payment reconciliation
[139] notifications
[140] reporting
[142] 3.5 Decision Support
[143] Management should have access to reliable information about:
[144] membership
[145] revenue
[146] claims
[147] utilization
[148] provider performance
[149] operational efficiency
[150] financial exposure
[151] complaints
[152] security events

[205] Chapter 5 — Stakeholder Model
[206] 5.1 Internal Stakeholders
[207] Executive Management
[208] Responsible for:
[209] strategic decisions
[210] organization-wide performance
[211] financial oversight
[212] operational oversight
[213] Operations
[214] Responsible for:
[215] enrollment
[216] member servicing
[217] provider operations
[218] daily HMO processes
[219] Claims Department
[220] Responsible for:
[221] claims intake
[222] validation
[223] assessment
[224] approval/rejection
[225] settlement workflows
[226] Medical Team
[227] Responsible for:
[228] medical review
[229] authorization
[230] utilization
[231] clinical assessment
[232] Finance
[233] Responsible for:
[234] billing
[235] premiums
[236] payments
[237] reconciliation
[238] financial reporting
[239] Provider Management
[240] Responsible for:
[241] provider onboarding
[242] credentialing
[243] contracts
[244] provider performance
[245] Customer Service
[246] Responsible for:
[247] member inquiries
[248] complaints
[249] service requests
[250] Compliance
[251] Responsible for:
[252] regulatory monitoring
[253] internal compliance
[254] audits
[255] policy enforcement
[256] Information Security
[257] Responsible for:
[258] security monitoring
[259] access control
[260] incident response
[261] security investigations
[262] IT
[263] Responsible for:
[264] infrastructure
[265] applications
[266] integrations
[267] technical support
[269] Chapter 6 — External Actors
[270] The system will support controlled external access for:
[271] 6.1 Members
[272] Members may:
[273] view profile
[274] view coverage
[275] view benefits
[276] view provider information
[277] view authorizations
[278] view claim status
[279] submit permitted requests
[280] communicate with support
[281] receive notifications
[283] 6.2 Employers
[284] Employer administrators may:
[285] manage employees
[286] submit enrollment data
[287] manage dependants
[288] view invoices
[289] view payment information
[290] access permitted reports
[291] manage authorized employer users
[292] Employer users must never see another employer's information.
[294] 6.3 Providers
[295] Providers may:
[296] verify eligibility
[297] request authorization
[298] submit claims
[299] track claims
[300] manage permitted facility information
[301] view applicable payment information
[302] Provider users must only access resources belonging to their provider organization.
[304] Chapter 7 — Multi-Organization Model
[305] The system should be designed for enterprise isolation.
[306] Conceptually:
[307] Platform
[308] │
[309] ├── HMO Organization
[310] │   │
[311] │   ├── Headquarters
[312] │   ├── Regions
[313] │   ├── Branches
[314] │   ├── Departments
[315] │   └── Employees
[316] │
[317] ├── Corporate Employers
[318] │
[319] ├── Provider Organizations
[320] │
[321] └── Members
[322] The authorization system must understand organizational boundaries.
[324] Chapter 8 — Invisible Pillars
[325] 8.1 Performance
[326] The platform must remain responsive under normal and peak operating conditions.
[327] Target objectives
[328] Targets will be refined during architecture and capacity planning.
[330] 8.2 Security
[331] Security is not a separate module.
[332] Security must exist across every module.
[333] Required principles:
[334] least privilege
[335] deny by default
[336] server-side authorization
[337] RBAC
[338] ABAC
[339] scope restrictions
[340] MFA
[341] encryption
[342] secure sessions
[343] audit trails
[344] security-event monitoring
[345] segregation of duties
[346] privileged access controls
[347] data isolation
[349] 8.3 Scalability
[350] The system should support growth in:
[351] members
[352] employers
[353] providers
[354] employees
[355] claims
[356] transactions
[357] documents
[358] API traffic
[359] reports
[360] integrations
[361] The architecture should allow horizontal scaling rather than relying solely on larger servers.
[363] 8.4 Reliability
[364] The system should tolerate:
[365] network failures
[366] service failures
[367] database failures
[368] third-party API failures
[369] payment failures
[370] notification failures
[371] message-queue failures
[372] Critical operations should use:
[373] retries
[374] idempotency
[375] circuit breakers
[376] transactional processing
[377] dead-letter queues
[378] monitoring
[379] recovery mechanisms
[381] 8.5 Usability
[382] The platform should present different interfaces according to the user's responsibility.
[383] A claims officer should not see a finance administrator's entire dashboard.
[384] A provider should not see internal HMO administrative functionality.
[385] A member should not see claims-management tools.
[386] The UI should therefore be:
[387] Permission-aware, role-aware and context-aware.
[389] Chapter 9 — Product Design Principles
[390] Principle 1 — Least Privilege
[391] Users receive only the permissions required to perform their responsibilities.
[393] Principle 2 — Deny by Default
[394] If the system cannot establish authorization:
[395] DENY
[397] Principle 3 — Separation of Duties
[398] Critical processes must be divided between appropriate users.
[399] Example:
[400] Claim Creator
[401] ↓
[402] Claim Reviewer
[403] ↓
[404] Claim Approver
[405] ↓
[406] Finance Processor
[407] ↓
[408] Payment Reconciliation
[409] The same person should not automatically be permitted to execute the entire chain.
[411] Principle 4 — No Frontend Authorization
[412] Hiding a button does not constitute security.
[413] The backend must independently verify every sensitive operation.
[415] Principle 5 — Every Sensitive Action Is Traceable
[416] Sensitive actions must generate appropriate audit/security events.
[418] Principle 6 — Access Is Contextual
[419] Authorization can depend on:
[420] user
[421] role
[422] department
[423] branch
[424] organization
[425] resource
[426] resource owner
[427] geographic scope
[428] approval limit
[429] transaction status
[430] time
[431] device
[432] risk level
[434] Principle 7 — Privileges Are Revocable
[435] Access must be capable of being revoked immediately.
[436] Examples:
[437] employee termination
[438] role change
[439] department transfer
[440] security incident
[441] temporary assignment expiry
[443] Principle 8 — Historical Integrity
[444] Important historical records must remain traceable.
[445] The platform must avoid destructive modifications that make it impossible to reconstruct what happened.

[578] Chapter 13 — Master Edge-Case Philosophy
[579] The platform will not only document known edge cases.
[580] It will also be designed to safely handle unknown edge cases.
[581] The principle is:
[582] An unexpected condition must fail safely, preserve data integrity, generate sufficient diagnostics, and never silently grant access.
[583] For every major operation, engineering teams must consider:
[584] VALID INPUT
[585] INVALID INPUT
[586] MISSING INPUT
[587] DUPLICATE INPUT
[588] CONCURRENT INPUT
[589] STALE INPUT
[590] MALICIOUS INPUT
[591] UNAUTHORIZED INPUT
[592] PARTIAL FAILURE
[593] TIMEOUT
[594] RETRY
[595] SYSTEM CRASH
[596] NETWORK FAILURE
[597] THIRD-PARTY FAILURE
[598] DATA CONFLICT
[599] STATE CONFLICT
[600] UNKNOWN CONDITION
[601] Unknown errors must result in controlled failure rather than undefined behavior.
[603] Chapter 14 — Master Authorization Philosophy
[604] Authorization is a platform-wide service.
[605] Every protected operation should conceptually execute:
[606] 1. Identify user
[607] 2. Validate session
[608] 3. Validate authentication
[609] 4. Resolve organization
[610] 5. Resolve roles
[611] 6. Resolve permissions
[612] 7. Resolve resource
[613] 8. Resolve data scope
[614] 9. Evaluate attributes
[615] 10. Evaluate business rules
[616] 11. Evaluate SoD
[617] 12. Evaluate approval requirements
[618] 13. Evaluate risk
[619] 14. Make authorization decision
[620] 15. Execute operation if permitted
[621] 16. Record audit event
[622] 17. Generate security event when required
[623] Possible outcomes:
[624] ALLOW
[625] DENY
[626] REQUIRE_MFA
[627] REQUIRE_APPROVAL
[628] REQUIRE_STEP_UP_AUTHENTICATION
[630] Chapter 15 — Blueprint Governance
[631] This Master Blueprint will become the reference point for:
[632] Product Management
[633] Business Analysis
[634] Solution Architecture
[635] UI/UX
[636] Backend Engineering
[637] Frontend Engineering
[638] Mobile Engineering
[639] Database Engineering
[640] DevOps
[641] QA
[642] Cybersecurity
[643] Compliance
[644] Operations
[645] Any future requirement should be evaluated against the blueprint.
[646] Changes should be version-controlled.
[647] Example:
[648] Blueprint v1.0
[649] ↓
[650] Requirement Change
[651] ↓
[652] Impact Analysis

[655] ↓

[660] Blueprint v1.1

[677] COMPLETE HMO MASTER BLUEPRINT
[678] PART II — HMO CORE MODULES
[680] Chapter 16 — Member Management
[681] 16.1 Purpose
[682] The Member Management module is the central repository for individuals covered by the HMO.
[683] It manages:
[684] members
[685] dependants
[686] member profiles
[687] eligibility
[688] coverage
[689] identification
[690] enrollment history
[691] status
[692] healthcare relationships
[693] documents
[694] communication preferences
[695] member service history
[697] 16.2 Member Types
[698] The system should support:
[699] Principal Member
[700] The primary person enrolled under an employer or individual plan.
[701] Dependant
[702] A person whose coverage is derived from a principal member.
[703] Examples may include:
[704] spouse
[705] child
[706] other permitted dependants
[707] Individual Member
[708] A member enrolled independently rather than through an employer.
[710] 16.3 Member Lifecycle
[711] PROSPECT
[712] ↓
[713] APPLICATION
[714] ↓
[715] PENDING VERIFICATION
[716] ↓
[717] VERIFIED
[718] ↓
[719] ENROLLED
[720] ↓
[721] ACTIVE
[722] ↓
[723] SUSPENDED
[724] ↓
[725] REACTIVATED
[726] ↓
[727] EXPIRED
[728] ↓
[729] TERMINATED
[730] ↓
[731] ARCHIVED
[732] Not every state transition should be permitted.
[733] For example:
[734] TERMINATED → ACTIVE
[735] should not happen directly.
[736] A new enrollment or approved reactivation process should be required.
[738] 16.4 Member Profile
[739] Core information should include:
[740] member ID
[741] policy/enrollment ID
[742] full name
[743] date of birth
[744] gender where required
[745] contact information
[746] residential information
[747] identification information
[748] employer
[749] plan
[750] coverage dates
[751] membership status
[752] principal/dependant relationship
[753] assigned provider where applicable
[754] emergency contact
[755] communication preferences
[756] Sensitive information must be appropriately protected and access-controlled.
[758] 16.5 Member Identification
[759] The platform should support configurable identification mechanisms.
[760] Examples:
[761] HMO membership number
[762] employer employee ID
[763] government-issued identity reference where legally and operationally appropriate
[764] provider verification reference
[765] QR-based membership verification
[766] The platform must distinguish:
[767] Member ID
[768] ≠
[769] User Account ID
[770] ≠
[771] Employer Employee ID
[772] ≠
[773] Provider Patient Reference
[775] 16.6 Member Duplicate Detection
[776] The system should detect possible duplicates using configurable matching criteria.
[777] Possible signals:
[778] name
[779] date of birth
[780] phone number
[781] email
[782] employer employee ID
[783] identity reference
[784] other approved identifiers
[785] The system should not automatically merge records solely because they appear similar.
[786] Possible workflow:
[787] Potential Duplicate
[788] ↓
[789] Review Queue
[790] ↓
[791] Authorized Reviewer
[792] ↓
[793] Confirm / Reject
[794] ↓
[795] Merge if approved
[796] ↓
[797] Audit Event
[799] 16.7 Member Merge Controls
[800] Member merging is a highly sensitive operation.
[801] Requirements:
[802] restricted permission
[803] mandatory reason
[804] approval where appropriate
[805] complete audit trail
[806] preservation of historical claims
[807] preservation of financial history
[808] preservation of medical-service history
[809] ability to identify the original records
[810] A merge must never silently destroy historical information.
[812] Chapter 17 — Dependant Management
[813] 17.1 Dependant Registration
[814] Authorized users should be able to register dependants according to applicable plan rules.
[815] The system should validate:
[816] relationship
[817] eligibility
[818] required documentation
[819] effective date
[820] principal member status
[822] 17.2 Dependant Lifecycle
[823] PROPOSED
[824] ↓
[825] DOCUMENTATION_PENDING
[826] ↓
[827] VERIFICATION
[828] ↓
[829] APPROVED
[830] ↓
[831] ACTIVE
[832] ↓
[833] SUSPENDED
[834] ↓
[835] EXPIRED
[836] ↓
[837] TERMINATED
[839] 17.3 Dependant Edge Cases
[840] The system must handle:
[841] dependant registered twice
[842] dependant linked to wrong principal
[843] dependant reaches eligibility limit
[844] principal member terminates
[845] dependant remains eligible under another rule
[846] missing documentation
[847] conflicting dates
[848] duplicate identity information
[849] dependant transferred between plans
[850] dependant termination reversal
[852] Chapter 18 — Employer / Corporate Client Management
[853] 18.1 Purpose
[854] This module manages organizations that sponsor healthcare coverage for employees.
[855] An employer may have:
[856] multiple branches
[857] departments
[858] employees
[859] dependants
[860] multiple plans
[861] multiple billing arrangements
[862] multiple authorized administrators
[864] 18.2 Employer Profile
[865] Fields may include:
[866] organization ID
[867] legal name
[868] trading name
[869] registration information
[870] contact details
[871] addresses
[872] industry
[873] billing information
[874] account manager
[875] contract
[876] plan assignments
[877] payment configuration
[878] status
[880] 18.3 Employer Lifecycle
[881] PROSPECT
[882] ↓
[883] ONBOARDING
[884] ↓
[885] VERIFICATION
[886] ↓
[887] CONTRACT_PENDING
[888] ↓
[889] ACTIVE
[890] ↓
[891] RENEWAL
[892] ↓
[893] SUSPENDED
[894] ↓
[895] TERMINATED
[896] ↓
[897] ARCHIVED
[899] 18.4 Employer User Access
[900] An employer may have multiple users:
[901] Employer
[902] │
[903] ├── Employer Admin
[904] ├── HR User
[905] ├── Finance User
[906] ├── Read-Only User
[907] └── Authorized Service User
[908] Each must have explicitly assigned permissions.
[909] Employer users must be restricted to their own organization.
[911] 18.5 Employer Isolation Rule
[912] Employer A must never access:
[913] Employer B's employees
[914] Employer B's invoices
[915] Employer B's claims
[916] Employer B's reports
[917] Employer B's documents
[918] even if the user has the same role.
[919] The authorization decision must include:
[920] organization_id
[921] as a mandatory security boundary.
[923] Chapter 19 — Employer Employee Management
[924] The platform should support:
[925] employee onboarding
[926] employee updates
[927] bulk upload
[928] bulk enrollment
[929] employee termination
[930] employee transfer
[931] dependant registration
[932] eligibility validation
[933] Bulk operations must be validated before committing.
[935] 19.1 Bulk Upload Architecture
[936] Upload File
[937] ↓
[938] Virus/Malware Scan
[939] ↓
[940] Format Validation
[941] ↓
[942] Schema Validation
[943] ↓
[944] Business Validation
[945] ↓
[946] Duplicate Detection
[947] ↓
[948] Preview
[949] ↓
[950] Employer Confirmation
[951] ↓
[952] Processing
[953] ↓
[954] Result Report
[955] The system should not partially process a file without clearly communicating which records succeeded and which failed.
[957] Chapter 20 — Provider Management
[958] 20.1 Purpose
[959] Provider Management controls the healthcare facilities and professionals available within the HMO network.
[960] Providers may include:
[961] hospitals
[962] clinics
[963] pharmacies
[964] laboratories
[965] diagnostic centers
[966] specialist facilities
[967] other approved healthcare organizations
[969] 20.2 Provider Profile
[970] Provider information should include:
[971] provider ID
[972] legal name
[973] facility name
[974] facility type
[975] location
[976] contact information
[977] services
[978] specialties
[979] accreditation
[980] licenses
[981] contract
[982] tariff
[983] bank/payment information
[984] operating status
[986] 20.3 Provider Lifecycle
[987] APPLICATION
[988] ↓
[989] DOCUMENT COLLECTION
[990] ↓
[991] CREDENTIAL VERIFICATION
[992] ↓
[993] ASSESSMENT
[994] ↓
[995] APPROVAL
[996] ↓
[997] CONTRACT NEGOTIATION
[998] ↓

[1000] ↓

[1004] ↓
[1005] REACTIVATED
[1006] ↓
[1007] TERMINATED
[1009] 20.4 Provider Onboarding
[1010] Provider onboarding should include:
[1011] Application
[1012] Documentation
[1013] Verification
[1014] Credential review
[1015] Service assessment
[1016] Contract
[1017] Tariff configuration
[1018] Approval
[1019] System activation
[1020] No provider should become active simply because a registration form was submitted.
[1022] Chapter 21 — Provider Credentialing
[1023] 21.1 Credential Types
[1024] The system should support configurable credential categories.
[1025] Examples:
[1026] professional licenses
[1027] facility certifications
[1028] accreditation
[1029] regulatory documents
[1030] insurance documentation
[1031] tax/business documentation
[1032] contractual documents
[1034] 21.2 Credential Expiry
[1035] Each credential should have:
[1036] issue date
[1037] expiry date
[1038] status
[1039] document
[1040] verification status
[1041] verification date
[1042] reviewer
[1043] The system should automatically generate alerts.
[1044] Example:
[1045] 90 days before expiry
[1046] ↓
[1047] Reminder
[1049] 60 days
[1050] ↓
[1051] Warning
[1053] 30 days
[1054] ↓
[1055] Escalation
[1057] Expired
[1058] ↓
[1059] Credential status = EXPIRED
[1060] Whether an expired credential automatically suspends a provider should be configurable according to business and regulatory rules.
[1062] 21.3 Provider Suspension
[1063] Possible triggers:
[1064] expired mandatory credential
[1065] regulatory restriction
[1066] contract expiry
[1067] compliance issue
[1068] management decision
[1069] fraud investigation
[1070] serious service-quality concern
[1071] Suspension should not automatically erase historical provider records.
[1073] Chapter 22 — Health Plan Management
[1074] 22.1 Purpose
[1075] Health Plan Management defines what members are entitled to receive.
[1076] A plan can contain:
[1077] benefits
[1078] exclusions
[1079] limits
[1080] co-pay rules
[1081] authorization requirements
[1082] provider network
[1083] effective dates
[1084] pricing
[1085] eligibility rules
[1087] 22.2 Plan Structure
[1088] PLAN
[1089] │
[1090] ├── Benefits
[1091] │
[1092] ├── Exclusions
[1093] │
[1094] ├── Limits
[1095] │
[1096] ├── Provider Network
[1097] │
[1098] ├── Pricing
[1099] │
[1100] ├── Eligibility Rules
[1101] │
[1102] └── Authorization Rules
[1104] 22.3 Plan Versioning
[1105] Plans must be versioned.
[1106] Example:
[1107] Plan Gold
[1108] ├── Version 1 — 2026
[1109] ├── Version 2 — 2027
[1110] └── Version 3 — 2028
[1111] Historical claims must use the benefit configuration applicable at the time of service.
[1112] A new plan configuration must not rewrite historical decisions.
[1114] 22.4 Benefit Rules
[1115] Benefits may contain:
[1116] service type
[1117] coverage percentage
[1118] maximum amount
[1119] frequency limit
[1120] annual limit
[1121] waiting period
[1122] co-pay
[1123] authorization requirement
[1124] provider restriction
[1126] 22.5 Benefit Example
[1127] Conceptually:
[1128] Benefit:
[1129] Specialist Consultation
[1131] Coverage:
[1132] 80%
[1134] Annual Limit:
[1135] 10 visits
[1137] Co-pay:
[1138] 20%
[1140] Authorization:
[1141] Not required
[1143] Provider:
[1144] Network providers only
[1146] Chapter 23 — Exclusions
[1147] Plans must support configurable exclusions.
[1148] Examples:
[1149] excluded service
[1150] excluded provider type
[1151] excluded condition/category where applicable
[1152] waiting period
[1153] annual limit reached
[1154] coverage expired
[1155] The system must explain the reason for a rejection rather than returning a generic:
[1156] "Not covered."
[1158] Chapter 24 — Enrollment Management
[1159] 24.1 Enrollment Sources
[1160] Enrollment may originate from:
[1161] employer
[1162] HMO employee
[1163] member self-service
[1164] API integration
[1165] approved bulk import
[1166] migration
[1168] 24.2 Enrollment Workflow
[1169] Enrollment Request
[1170] ↓
[1171] Identity Validation
[1172] ↓
[1173] Eligibility Validation
[1174] ↓
[1175] Plan Selection
[1176] ↓
[1177] Documentation
[1178] ↓
[1179] Approval
[1180] ↓
[1181] Enrollment Created
[1182] ↓
[1183] Membership Activated
[1184] ↓
[1185] Notification
[1187] 24.3 Enrollment Effective Dates
[1188] The system must support:
[1189] immediate enrollment
[1190] future-dated enrollment
[1191] backdated enrollment where authorized
[1192] scheduled termination
[1193] renewal
[1194] Backdating must require additional authorization because it can affect claims and financial liabilities.
[1196] 24.4 Enrollment Edge Cases
[1197] enrollment overlaps another enrollment
[1198] member enrolled in two incompatible plans
[1199] invalid effective date
[1200] future plan not yet active
[1201] employer contract expired
[1202] missing premium
[1203] duplicate employee
[1204] dependant exceeds eligibility
[1205] enrollment approved after service date
[1206] enrollment cancelled after claims exist
[1208] Chapter 25 — Eligibility Management
[1209] Eligibility should be evaluated dynamically.
[1210] Conceptually:
[1211] Is Member Active?
[1212] +
[1213] Is Coverage Effective?
[1214] +
[1215] Is Service Covered?
[1216] +
[1217] Is Provider Eligible?
[1218] +
[1219] Are Benefit Limits Available?
[1220] +
[1221] Are Required Authorizations Present?
[1222] ↓
[1223] ELIGIBILITY DECISION
[1224] Possible responses:
[1225] ELIGIBLE
[1226] PARTIALLY_ELIGIBLE
[1227] NOT_ELIGIBLE
[1228] REQUIRES_AUTHORIZATION
[1229] REQUIRES_REVIEW
[1231] Chapter 26 — Eligibility Verification
[1232] Providers should be able to perform controlled eligibility verification.
[1233] The response should expose only information necessary for the provider's legitimate purpose.
[1234] For example:
[1235] Member:
[1236] Active
[1238] Plan:
[1239] Gold
[1241] Coverage:
[1242] Active
[1244] Service:
[1245] Specialist Consultation
[1247] Result:
[1248] Eligible
[1249] The provider should not automatically receive unrestricted access to the member's entire record.
[1251] Chapter 27 — Claims Management
[1252] 27.1 Purpose
[1253] Claims Management handles the complete lifecycle of healthcare claims.
[1255] 27.2 Claim Sources

[1257] provider portal
[1258] API
[1259] authorized internal user
[1260] approved bulk submission
[1261] integrated provider system

[1264] DRAFT
[1265] ↓

[1285] ↓

[1287] ↓
[1288] PAID
[1289] ↓
[1290] RECONCILED

[1292] disputed
[1293] resubmitted
[1294] cancelled
[1295] reversed
[1296] under investigation
[1298] Chapter 28 — Claim Validation
[1299] Every claim should undergo:
[1300] Structural validation
[1301] required fields
[1302] correct formats
[1303] valid references
[1304] Business validation
[1305] member eligibility
[1306] provider status
[1307] service coverage
[1308] tariff
[1309] benefit limits
[1310] Duplicate validation
[1311] duplicate claim
[1312] duplicate invoice
[1313] duplicate service
[1314] Financial validation
[1315] amount
[1316] tariff
[1317] co-pay
[1318] payment rules
[1319] Fraud/risk validation
[1320] suspicious patterns
[1321] abnormal frequency
[1322] unusual amounts
[1324] Chapter 29 — Claims Review
[1325] Claims review should support:
[1326] automated review
[1327] manual review
[1328] medical review
[1329] financial review
[1330] fraud review
[1331] The system should route claims according to configurable rules.
[1332] Example:
[1333] Claim < ₦100,000
[1334] ↓
[1335] Standard workflow
[1337] Claim ≥ ₦100,000
[1338] ↓
[1339] Senior review
[1341] High-risk claim
[1342] ↓
[1343] Fraud/Medical review
[1344] Thresholds must be configurable.
[1346] Chapter 30 — Claims Approval
[1347] Approval permissions should be based on:
[1348] role
[1349] department
[1350] amount
[1351] claim type
[1352] provider
[1353] risk
[1354] geographic scope
[1355] plan
[1356] authorization level
[1357] Example:
[1358] Claims Officer
[1359] → Review
[1361] Claims Supervisor
[1362] → Approve within assigned limit
[1364] Claims Manager
[1365] → Higher approval limit
[1367] Executive/Authorized Approver
[1368] → Exceptional threshold
[1370] Chapter 31 — Claim Segregation of Duties
[1371] A configurable rule should prevent:
[1372] User A
[1373] Creates Claim
[1375] User A
[1376] Approves Claim
[1378] User A
[1379] Authorizes Payment
[1380] for transactions requiring separation.
[1381] Instead:
[1382] Creator
[1383] ↓
[1384] Reviewer
[1385] ↓
[1386] Approver
[1387] ↓
[1388] Finance
[1389] ↓
[1390] Payment
[1392] Chapter 32 — Claim Rejection
[1393] Every rejection must have:
[1394] rejection reason
[1395] rule/code
[1396] reviewer
[1397] timestamp
[1398] supporting information
[1399] appeal/resubmission status where applicable
[1400] The system should distinguish:
[1401] REJECTED
[1402] from:
[1403] RETURNED_FOR_CORRECTION
[1404] A correctable data issue should not necessarily be treated as a final rejection.
[1406] Chapter 33 — Claim Resubmission
[1407] A provider should be able to resubmit eligible rejected/returned claims according to configured rules.
[1408] The system must retain:
[1409] Original Claim
[1410] ↓
[1411] Reason for Return/Rejection
[1412] ↓
[1413] Correction
[1414] ↓
[1415] Resubmission
[1416] ↓
[1417] New Review
[1418] Historical versions must remain traceable.
[1420] Chapter 34 — Preauthorization
[1421] Certain services may require authorization before treatment.
[1422] Workflow
[1423] Provider
[1424] ↓
[1425] Authorization Request
[1426] ↓
[1427] Member Eligibility
[1428] ↓
[1429] Benefit Check
[1430] ↓
[1431] Clinical Review
[1432] ↓
[1433] Decision
[1434] ↓
[1435] Authorization Number
[1436] ↓
[1437] Provider

[1443] ↓

[1451] ↓

[1477] ↓

[1486] service

[1489] Chapter 36 — Utilization Management
[1490] Utilization Management monitors healthcare usage.

[1492] service frequency

[1502] pharmacy providers

[1512] Chapter 38 — Laboratory Management
[1513] The platform may support:
[1514] laboratory providers
[1515] test catalog

[1517] referrals
[1518] result references
[1519] claims
[1520] utilization
[1521] Sensitive test results should require appropriate authorization and should not be exposed to users who do not need them.
[1523] Chapter 39 — Medical Service Management
[1524] Services should have standardized internal identifiers.

[1526] Consultation
[1527] Laboratory

[1531] Pharmacy

[1541] coverage rule

[1552] ↓

[1554] ↓

[1556] ↓
[1557] CLOSED
[1558] Possible additional states:
[1559] escalated
[1560] reopened
[1561] withdrawn
[1562] rejected as invalid
[1564] Chapter 41 — Complaint Controls
[1565] Each complaint should have:
[1566] complaint ID
[1567] complainant
[1568] category
[1569] priority
[1570] description
[1571] supporting documents
[1572] assigned officer
[1573] SLA
[1574] escalation level
[1575] resolution
[1576] closure reason
[1577] A user should not be able to close their own complaint investigation where segregation-of-duties rules prohibit it.
[1579] Chapter 42 — Customer Support
[1580] Customer service should support:
[1581] member inquiries
[1582] claim inquiries
[1583] eligibility inquiries
[1584] provider issues
[1585] enrollment issues
[1586] billing questions
[1587] complaints
[1588] service requests
[1589] Customer-service users should receive minimum necessary access.
[1590] For example:
[1591] A customer-service agent may verify:
[1592] Member active: YES
[1593] Plan: GOLD
[1594] Coverage: ACTIVE
[1595] without being allowed to edit medical information.
[1597] Chapter 43 — Document Management
[1598] Documents may be attached to:
[1599] members
[1600] dependants
[1601] employers
[1602] providers
[1603] claims
[1604] authorizations
[1605] complaints
[1606] contracts
[1607] invoices
[1609] 43.1 Document Security
[1610] Documents require:
[1611] access control
[1612] encryption
[1613] malware scanning
[1614] metadata
[1615] versioning
[1616] audit logging
[1617] retention rules
[1618] expiry monitoring
[1620] 43.2 Document Access
[1621] A document should inherit or explicitly define access restrictions.
[1622] For example:
[1623] Provider License
[1624] → Provider Management
[1626] Medical Claim Document
[1627] → Authorized Claims/Medical Users
[1629] Employer Contract
[1630] → Authorized Employer/Legal/Finance Users
[1631] A generic "document access" permission should not automatically expose every document.
[1633] Chapter 44 — Cross-Module Business Rules
[1634] The modules must not operate as isolated databases.
[1635] Example:
[1636] Member
[1637] ↓
[1638] Enrollment
[1639] ↓
[1640] Plan
[1641] ↓
[1642] Benefit
[1643] ↓
[1644] Provider
[1645] ↓
[1646] Authorization
[1647] ↓
[1648] Service
[1649] ↓
[1650] Claim
[1651] ↓
[1652] Approval
[1653] ↓
[1654] Payment
[1655] ↓
[1656] Reconciliation
[1657] Every stage should reference authoritative records from the appropriate module.
[1659] Chapter 45 — Cross-Module State Integrity
[1660] The system must prevent impossible states.
[1661] Example:
[1662] Member = TERMINATED
[1664] Claim = NEW
[1666] Service Date = After Termination
[1667] The claim must be evaluated against applicable coverage rules and should not automatically proceed as a normal eligible claim.
[1668] Another example:
[1669] Provider = SUSPENDED
[1671] New Claim = SUBMITTED
[1672] The system must apply the configured provider-status rules before processing.
[1674] Chapter 46 — Critical Edge-Case Matrix
[1676] Chapter 47 — Unknown Edge-Case Handling
[1677] The platform must assume that not every failure can be predicted during product design.
[1678] Therefore every major service must implement:
[1679] Validation
[1680] +
[1681] Authorization
[1682] +
[1683] Transaction Integrity
[1684] +
[1685] Idempotency
[1686] +
[1687] State Validation
[1688] +
[1689] Error Handling
[1690] +
[1691] Logging
[1692] +
[1693] Audit
[1694] +
[1695] Monitoring
[1696] +
[1697] Recovery
[1698] Unexpected conditions must never result in:
[1699] accidental access
[1700] duplicate payment
[1701] corrupted member record
[1702] lost claim
[1703] silent state transition
[1704] missing audit trail
[1706] Chapter 48 — Core HMO State-Machine Principle
[1707] Every important entity must have a controlled lifecycle.
[1708] The system should never permit arbitrary status changes.
[1709] For example:
[1710] ACTIVE → SUSPENDED
[1711] may be permitted.
[1712] But:
[1713] ACTIVE → PAID
[1714] for a provider is nonsensical and must be rejected by domain validation.
[1715] State transitions must therefore be:
[1716] explicit
[1717] validated
[1718] authorized
[1719] auditable
[1720] atomic
[1722] Part II Completion Criteria
[1723] The HMO Core Modules are considered defined when the following have been established:
[1724] member lifecycle
[1725] dependant lifecycle
[1726] employer lifecycle
[1727] provider lifecycle
[1728] credential lifecycle
[1729] plan lifecycle
[1730] enrollment lifecycle
[1731] eligibility rules
[1732] claims lifecycle
[1733] preauthorization lifecycle
[1734] referral lifecycle
[1735] utilization processes
[1736] pharmacy processes
[1737] laboratory processes
[1738] complaint lifecycle
[1739] customer-support processes
[1740] document-management controls
[1741] cross-module dependencies
[1742] core business rules
[1743] initial edge-case catalogue
[1744] unknown-failure strategy
[1745] These definitions will be used directly in the next parts of the blueprint.
[1746] COMPLETE HMO MASTER BLUEPRINT
[1747] PART III — FINANCIAL OPERATIONS
[1749] Chapter 49 — Financial Operations Overview
[1750] 49.1 Purpose
[1751] The Financial Operations module manages all financial activity associated with the HMO platform.
[1752] It covers:
[1753] premiums
[1754] invoices
[1755] receivables
[1756] payments
[1757] provider settlements
[1758] claims financial processing
[1759] refunds
[1760] adjustments
[1761] credit/debit notes
[1762] reconciliation
[1763] financial approvals
[1764] financial reporting
[1765] financial audit
[1766] financial fraud detection
[1767] The financial subsystem must be treated as a high-integrity system.
[1768] Financial records must never depend solely on mutable application records.
[1770] Chapter 50 — Financial Architecture Principles
[1771] The financial system must follow these principles:
[1772] 1. No silent financial changes
[1773] A financial transaction must never be modified without an audit trail.
[1774] 2. No destructive financial history
[1775] Posted transactions should generally be reversed or adjusted rather than deleted.
[1776] 3. Double-entry accounting
[1777] Financially significant transactions should be represented using balanced accounting entries.
[1778] 4. Idempotency
[1779] The same payment request must not create multiple financial transactions.
[1780] 5. Segregation of duties
[1781] Creation, approval, execution and reconciliation should be separated where required.
[1782] 6. Approval limits
[1783] Users should have configurable transaction approval limits.
[1784] 7. Complete auditability
[1785] Every significant financial action must be traceable.
[1786] 8. Reconciliation
[1787] Internal records must be reconciled against external payment/bank records.
[1789] Chapter 51 — Financial Organization Structure
[1790] The financial module should support:
[1791] HMO
[1792] │
[1793] ├── Finance Department
[1794] │   ├── Accounts Receivable
[1795] │   ├── Accounts Payable
[1796] │   ├── Treasury
[1797] │   ├── Reconciliation
[1798] │   └── Financial Reporting
[1799] │
[1800] ├── Branches
[1801] │
[1802] └── Cost Centers
[1803] Financial permissions should be scoped accordingly.
[1805] Chapter 52 — Financial Roles
[1806] Example roles:
[1807] Important: A system administrator must not automatically receive permission to approve or execute financial transactions.
[1809] Chapter 53 — Premium Management
[1810] 53.1 Purpose
[1811] Premium Management manages payments owed by members or sponsoring organizations for healthcare coverage.
[1812] Premium structures may include:
[1813] employer-sponsored premiums
[1814] individual premiums
[1815] dependant premiums
[1816] recurring premiums
[1817] scheduled premiums
[1818] adjustments
[1819] discounts where authorized
[1821] Chapter 54 — Premium Configuration
[1822] Premium rules may depend on:
[1823] plan
[1824] member category
[1825] dependant category
[1826] employer contract
[1827] coverage period
[1828] employee class
[1829] geographic arrangement
[1830] negotiated pricing
[1831] Example:
[1832] Employer
[1833] ↓
[1834] Plan
[1835] ↓
[1836] Employee Category
[1837] ↓
[1838] Premium Rule
[1839] ↓
[1840] Coverage Period
[1841] ↓
[1842] Amount Due
[1844] Chapter 55 — Premium Lifecycle
[1845] CALCULATED
[1846] ↓
[1847] GENERATED
[1848] ↓
[1849] INVOICED
[1850] ↓
[1851] PARTIALLY_PAID
[1852] ↓

[1856] Alternative states:
[1857] OVERDUE

[1864] Chapter 56 — Billing & Invoicing
[1865] The system should generate invoices for:
[1866] employer premiums
[1867] individual plans
[1868] additional dependants
[1869] approved services where applicable
[1870] other configured HMO charges
[1871] Invoices should include:
[1872] invoice number
[1873] customer
[1874] billing period
[1875] line items
[1876] quantity
[1877] unit price
[1878] tax where applicable
[1879] discount
[1880] total
[1881] due date
[1882] status
[1883] payment reference
[1885] Chapter 57 — Invoice Numbering
[1886] Invoice numbers must be:
[1887] unique
[1888] sequential where required by business policy
[1889] non-colliding
[1890] auditable
[1891] generated server-side
[1892] The system must prevent two concurrent requests from generating the same invoice number.

[1895] DRAFT
[1896] ↓
[1897] PENDING_APPROVAL
[1898] ↓
[1899] APPROVED
[1900] ↓
[1901] ISSUED
[1902] ↓
[1903] PARTIALLY_PAID
[1904] ↓
[1905] PAID
[1906] ↓
[1907] OVERDUE
[1908] ↓
[1909] DISPUTED
[1910] ↓
[1911] CLOSED
[1912] Other possible states:
[1913] cancelled
[1914] voided
[1915] written-off

[1922] payment promises
[1923] disputed invoices
[1924] aging
[1925] collection status

[1929] 31–60 days
[1930] 61–90 days
[1931] 91–120 days
[1932] 120+ days

[1936] The system should automatically identify:

[1955] Chapter 62 — Payment Lifecycle
[1956] INITIATED
[1957] ↓
[1958] PENDING
[1959] ↓
[1960] PROCESSING

[1962] SUCCESSFUL
[1963] ↓
[1964] RECONCILING
[1965] ↓
[1966] RECONCILED
[1967] Failure states:
[1968] FAILED
[1969] TIMEOUT
[1970] CANCELLED
[1971] REVERSED
[1972] DISPUTED
[1974] Chapter 63 — Payment Idempotency
[1975] Every payment initiation should support an idempotency key.
[1976] Example:
[1977] Payment Request
[1978] Idempotency Key:
[1979] PAY-EMP001-20261001-00001
[1980] If the same request is received again:
[1981] Existing transaction found
[1982] ↓
[1983] Do not create second payment
[1984] ↓
[1985] Return original transaction state
[1986] This protects against:
[1987] browser retries
[1988] network retries
[1989] gateway retries
[1990] duplicated API requests
[1991] message duplication
[1993] Chapter 64 — Unknown Payment Status
[1994] A particularly important edge case is:
[1995] HMO → Payment Gateway
[1996] ↓
[1997] Request sent
[1998] ↓
[1999] Network timeout
[2000] ↓
[2001] No response
[2002] The system must not assume failure.
[2003] Instead:
[2004] UNKNOWN
[2005] ↓
[2006] Query gateway
[2007] ↓
[2008] Webhook reconciliation
[2009] ↓
[2010] Final state
[2011] Possible final outcomes:
[2012] SUCCESS
[2013] FAILED
[2014] REVERSED
[2015] UNKNOWN → MANUAL REVIEW
[2017] Chapter 65 — Payment Webhooks
[2018] Payment providers may send asynchronous notifications.
[2019] The system must:
[2020] Authenticate the webhook.
[2021] Validate its signature.
[2022] Validate the event.
[2023] Check idempotency.
[2024] Locate the transaction.
[2025] Validate the state transition.
[2026] Update transaction state.
[2027] Create accounting entries.
[2028] Record an audit event.
[2029] Duplicate webhooks must not duplicate payments.
[2031] Chapter 66 — Provider Settlement

[2034] approved claims
[2035] contract tariffs
[2036] negotiated rates
[2037] deductions
[2038] adjustments
[2039] disputed claims
[2040] withholding rules where applicable

[2088] approver

[2092] Chapter 70 — Financial Approval Matrix
[2093] The platform should support configurable approval thresholds.
[2094] Example:
[2095] These numbers are configuration examples, not fixed business rules.
[2096] The actual HMO organization should configure its own limits.
[2098] Chapter 71 — Dynamic Approval Rules
[2099] Approval requirements may depend on more than amount.
[2100] Example:
[2101] IF
[2102] amount > threshold
[2103] OR
[2104] provider = high-risk
[2105] OR
[2106] transaction_type = refund
[2107] OR
[2108] transaction = exceptional adjustment
[2109] OR
[2110] risk_score = HIGH
[2111] THEN
[2112] additional approval required
[2114] Chapter 72 — Segregation of Duties
[2115] Financial workflows must prevent dangerous combinations.
[2116] Example:
[2117] Create Payment
[2118] ↓
[2119] Approve Payment
[2120] ↓
[2121] Execute Payment
[2122] ↓
[2123] Reconcile Payment
[2124] A single user should not automatically perform all four stages.
[2126] Chapter 73 — Self-Approval Prevention
[2127] The system must detect:
[2128] User creates refund
[2129] ↓
[2130] Same user attempts approval
[2131] ↓
[2132] DENIED
[2133] This should generate an appropriate audit event.
[2134] Repeated attempts may also generate a security event.
[2136] Chapter 74 — Refund Management
[2137] Refunds may arise from:
[2138] overpayment
[2139] cancelled transaction
[2140] duplicate payment
[2141] approved billing correction
[2142] service cancellation
[2143] other approved financial reasons
[2144] Refunds should require:
[2145] reason
[2146] original transaction
[2147] amount
[2148] requester
[2149] reviewer
[2150] approver
[2151] payment destination
[2152] audit trail
[2154] Chapter 75 — Refund Controls
[2155] A refund must not exceed the refundable balance.
[2156] Example:
[2157] Original payment = ₦500,000
[2159] Already refunded = ₦100,000
[2161] Maximum remaining refund = ₦400,000
[2162] The system must enforce this server-side.
[2164] Chapter 76 — Financial Adjustments
[2165] Adjustments may include:
[2166] debit adjustment
[2167] credit adjustment
[2168] tariff correction
[2169] invoice correction
[2170] settlement correction
[2171] accounting correction
[2172] Posted transactions should generally be corrected through controlled adjustment entries rather than editing historical records.
[2174] Chapter 77 — Credit & Debit Notes
[2175] The platform should support:
[2176] Credit Note
[2177] Reduces amount owed.
[2178] Debit Note
[2179] Increases amount owed.
[2180] Each should reference the original transaction.
[2182] Chapter 78 — Write-Offs
[2183] Write-offs are highly sensitive.
[2184] Requirements:
[2185] reason
[2186] amount
[2187] supporting evidence
[2188] authorization level
[2189] approval
[2190] audit event
[2191] financial reporting
[2192] A normal finance officer should not automatically have write-off authority.
[2194] Chapter 79 — Reconciliation
[2195] Reconciliation compares internal records against external financial records.

[2197] bank statements
[2198] payment gateway records
[2199] provider payment reports
[2200] accounting systems

[2254] Transaction
[2255] ↓
[2256] Journal Entry
[2257] ↓

[2259] ↓

[2262] TOTAL DEBITS = TOTAL CREDITS
[2264] Chapter 84 — Chart of Accounts

[2312] Chapter 87 — Financial Fraud Controls
[2313] The system should detect patterns such as:
[2314] Duplicate payment
[2315] Same transaction processed multiple times.
[2316] Split transactions
[2317] A large transaction deliberately divided to avoid approval thresholds.
[2318] Example:
[2319] ₦9,900,000
[2320] ₦9,900,000
[2321] ₦9,900,000
[2322] where the configured approval threshold is ₦10 million.
[2323] This should be detectable as a pattern rather than evaluated only as individual transactions.
[2325] Chapter 88 — Other Financial Risk Signals
[2326] Potential signals include:
[2327] unusual refund frequency
[2328] sudden bank-account change
[2329] new provider receiving unusually large settlement
[2330] repeated manual adjustments
[2331] excessive write-offs
[2332] unusual transaction timing
[2333] repeated failed payment attempts
[2334] multiple transactions from unusual devices
[2335] abnormal approval patterns
[2336] A risk signal should trigger investigation or additional controls according to policy; it should not by itself be treated as proof of wrongdoing.
[2338] Chapter 89 — Provider Bank Account Changes
[2339] Provider payment information is highly sensitive.
[2340] A change should require:
[2341] Authorized request
[2342] Verification
[2343] Approval
[2344] Audit trail
[2345] Effective date
[2346] Optional cooling-off period where business policy requires it
[2347] Existing payment batches should retain the bank details applicable at the time of authorization.
[2349] Chapter 90 — Financial Access Control
[2350] Financial permissions should be granular.
[2351] Examples:

[2354] finance.invoice.update
[2355] finance.invoice.approve
[2356] finance.invoice.cancel

[2365] finance.refund.approve

[2373] finance.writeoff.approve

[2375] finance.payment.read
[2376] must not automatically receive:
[2377] finance.payment.execute
[2379] Chapter 91 — Financial Data Scope
[2380] Access can be restricted by:
[2381] branch

[2391] Finance Officer
[2392] +
[2393] Payment Read
[2394] +
[2395] Branch Lagos
[2396] does not imply access to payments belonging to another branch.
[2398] Chapter 92 — Financial Transaction State Protection
[2399] Every financial state transition must be validated.
[2400] For example:
[2401] PAID → PENDING
[2402] should not be allowed as an ordinary update.
[2403] A reversal or correction workflow should be required.
[2405] Chapter 93 — Concurrency Protection
[2406] Consider:
[2407] User A opens invoice
[2408] User B opens invoice
[2410] User A applies adjustment
[2411] User B applies adjustment
[2412] The system must prevent silent overwriting.
[2413] Possible mechanisms:
[2414] optimistic locking
[2415] row versioning
[2416] transactional locks
[2417] conflict detection

[2447] duplicate refund

[2449] refund to changed account
[2450] refund approval conflict
[2451] Reconciliation

[2454] amount mismatch
[2455] duplicate external transaction
[2456] delayed bank settlement
[2458] Chapter 95 — Unknown Financial Failure Strategy
[2459] If an unexpected financial condition occurs:
[2460] STOP unsafe operation
[2461] ↓
[2462] Preserve transaction state
[2463] ↓
[2464] Generate correlation ID
[2465] ↓
[2466] Log technical error
[2467] ↓
[2468] Generate audit/security event where applicable
[2469] ↓
[2470] Place transaction in controlled exception state
[2471] ↓
[2472] Notify responsible team
[2473] ↓
[2474] Permit controlled recovery
[2475] The system must never respond to uncertainty by:
[2476] "Try the payment again."
[2477] without first checking whether the original transaction succeeded.

[2602] COMPLETE HMO MASTER BLUEPRINT
[2603] PART IV — ENTERPRISE ACCESS CONTROL & IDENTITY ARCHITECTURE
[2604] 4.0 Purpose
[2605] The Enterprise Access Control & Identity Architecture defines how every person, organization, system, service, device, API, and automated process is authenticated, authorized, monitored, restricted, and audited across the HMO platform.
[2606] Because the HMO platform handles sensitive member information, medical information, financial transactions, provider information, employer information, claims, approvals, and operational records, access control is a core business architecture, not merely a security feature.
[2607] The fundamental rule is:
[2608] No user, service, API, or process receives access simply because it exists. Every access decision must be explicitly authorized.
[2609] The authorization model shall combine:
[2610] RBAC — Role-Based Access Control
[2611] ABAC — Attribute-Based Access Control
[2612] Scope-Based Access Control
[2613] Segregation of Duties
[2614] Approval Workflows
[2615] Risk-Based Controls
[2616] Data Classification
[2617] Field-Level Restrictions
[2618] Temporary Access
[2619] Privileged Access Management
[2620] Auditability
[2622] 4.1 Access Control Objectives
[2623] The architecture shall ensure that:
[2624] Users can only access resources they are authorized to access.
[2625] Users can only perform actions permitted by their privileges.
[2626] Users cannot bypass authorization through APIs.
[2627] Users cannot access another organization or tenant's data.
[2628] Sensitive medical and financial information receives additional controls.
[2629] High-risk operations require stronger authentication and/or approval.
[2630] Users cannot approve their own transactions where separation of duties applies.
[2631] Privileges can be revoked immediately.
[2632] Temporary privileges automatically expire.
[2633] Every sensitive authorization decision can be traced.
[2634] Administrative privileges are tightly controlled.
[2635] Automated services receive only the permissions they require.
[2636] Access decisions are consistent across web, mobile, API, and background processing.
[2637] Failed authorization defaults to DENY.
[2639] 4.2 Core Authorization Model
[2640] Every request follows this conceptual chain:
[2641] REQUEST
[2642] ↓
[2643] IDENTITY
[2644] ↓
[2645] AUTHENTICATION
[2646] ↓
[2647] ORGANIZATION CONTEXT
[2648] ↓
[2649] ROLE
[2650] ↓
[2651] PERMISSION
[2652] ↓
[2653] RESOURCE
[2654] ↓
[2655] ACTION
[2656] ↓
[2657] DATA SCOPE
[2658] ↓
[2659] ATTRIBUTES
[2660] ↓
[2661] BUSINESS RULES
[2662] ↓
[2663] RISK EVALUATION
[2664] ↓
[2665] SEGREGATION OF DUTIES
[2666] ↓
[2667] APPROVAL REQUIREMENT
[2668] ↓
[2669] AUTHORIZATION DECISION
[2670] ↓
[2671] AUDIT EVENT
[2672] Possible decisions:
[2673] ALLOW
[2674] DENY
[2675] ALLOW_WITH_MASKING
[2676] ALLOW_WITH_APPROVAL
[2677] ALLOW_WITH_STEP_UP_AUTHENTICATION
[2678] ALLOW_WITH_RESTRICTIONS
[2679] The default must always be:
[2680] NO_MATCHING_POLICY = DENY
[2682] 4.3 Identity Architecture
[2683] The system shall distinguish between:
[2684] Human identities
[2685] HMO employees
[2686] Administrators
[2687] Finance staff
[2688] Claims officers
[2689] Medical officers
[2690] Customer service officers
[2691] Provider users
[2692] Employer users
[2693] Auditors
[2694] Compliance users
[2695] Executives
[2696] External identities
[2697] Members
[2698] Dependants where applicable
[2699] Employer representatives
[2700] Provider representatives
[2701] Third-party integration users
[2702] Machine identities
[2703] Microservices
[2704] Background workers
[2705] Scheduled jobs
[2706] API clients
[2707] Payment gateways
[2708] Notification services
[2709] Analytics services
[2710] Integration partners
[2711] A machine identity must never be treated as an ordinary employee account.
[2713] 4.4 Identity Lifecycle
[2714] Every human identity shall follow a controlled lifecycle.
[2715] REQUESTED
[2716] ↓
[2717] PENDING_VERIFICATION
[2718] ↓
[2719] ACTIVE
[2720] ↓
[2721] SUSPENDED
[2722] ↓
[2723] REACTIVATED
[2724] ↓
[2725] DEACTIVATED
[2726] ↓
[2727] ARCHIVED
[2728] Additional states may include:
[2729] LOCKED
[2730] PENDING_MFA
[2731] PENDING_APPROVAL
[2732] COMPROMISED
[2733] PENDING_TERMINATION
[2734] Identity creation
[2735] Creating an employee identity should require:
[2736] Employee identification
[2737] Name
[2738] Email
[2739] Phone
[2740] Organization
[2741] Branch
[2742] Department
[2743] Job function
[2744] Employment status
[2745] Assigned role
[2746] Required access scope
[2747] Manager
[2748] Identity verification
[2749] MFA enrollment
[2750] The system should prevent creation of duplicate identities.
[2752] 4.5 Identity Correlation
[2753] The platform must distinguish:
[2754] User ID
[2755] Employee ID
[2756] Member ID
[2757] Employer Employee ID
[2758] Provider User ID
[2759] Organization ID
[2760] Role ID
[2761] Session ID
[2762] Device ID
[2763] These identifiers must never be casually interchangeable.
[2764] Example:
[2765] User ID:
[2766] USR-000123
[2768] Employee ID:
[2769] EMP-004521
[2771] Member ID:
[2772] MEM-839291
[2774] Organization ID:
[2775] ORG-00001
[2776] This prevents identity confusion and reduces the risk of authorization errors.
[2778] 4.6 Authentication Architecture
[2779] Authentication shall support appropriate methods based on user type and risk.
[2780] Possible mechanisms include:
[2781] Username/password
[2782] Email/password
[2783] Phone/OTP
[2784] Authenticator application
[2785] Passkeys where supported
[2786] Biometric authentication through supported devices
[2787] SSO
[2788] Enterprise identity providers
[2789] Service credentials
[2790] OAuth-based authentication
[2791] Certificate-based service authentication where required
[2792] Passwords must never be stored in plaintext.
[2794] 4.7 Multi-Factor Authentication
[2795] MFA should be mandatory for privileged and sensitive users.
[2796] Examples:
[2797] Standard user
[2798] Password + OTP
[2799] Finance user
[2800] Password/SSO + MFA
[2801] System administrator
[2802] Strong authentication + MFA + privileged session controls
[2803] High-risk financial action
[2804] Existing authenticated session
[2805] +
[2806] Step-up authentication
[2807] +
[2808] Approval
[2810] 4.8 Step-Up Authentication
[2811] Certain operations must trigger additional authentication even when the user is already logged in.
[2812] Examples:
[2813] Changing bank account information
[2814] Approving high-value payment
[2815] Approving large provider settlement
[2816] Exporting sensitive member data
[2817] Changing another user's privileges
[2818] Creating privileged accounts
[2819] Disabling security controls
[2820] Executing emergency access
[2821] Performing mass data export
[2822] Example:
[2823] User authenticated
[2824] ↓
[2825] Attempts ₦5,000,000 settlement approval
[2826] ↓
[2827] Risk engine evaluates
[2828] ↓
[2829] Step-up authentication
[2830] ↓
[2831] Approval policy
[2832] ↓
[2833] Authorization
[2835] 4.9 Session Management
[2836] Every authenticated session shall have:
[2837] Session ID
[2838] User ID
[2839] Device identifier
[2840] Authentication timestamp
[2841] Last activity
[2842] IP information where appropriate
[2843] Authentication method
[2844] MFA state
[2845] Risk state
[2846] Session expiration
[2847] Revocation status
[2848] The system must support immediate session revocation.
[2849] Example:
[2850] Employee terminated
[2851] ↓
[2852] Identity disabled
[2853] ↓
[2854] All active sessions revoked
[2855] ↓
[2856] Access tokens invalidated/blocked
[2857] ↓
[2858] Active privileged sessions terminated
[2860] 4.10 Device Management
[2861] The platform may maintain a trusted-device registry.
[2862] Device attributes may include:
[2863] Device ID
[2864] Device type
[2865] OS
[2866] Browser/application version
[2867] First seen
[2868] Last seen
[2869] Trust status
[2870] Risk status
[2871] User association
[2872] Suspicious devices may trigger:
[2873] Additional verification
[2874] Restricted access
[2875] Session termination
[2876] Security alert
[2877] Account lock
[2879] 4.11 RBAC Architecture
[2880] RBAC determines what a user is generally allowed to do based on their role.
[2881] Example:
[2882] Claims Officer
[2883] ↓
[2884] Claims permissions
[2885] However:
[2886] A role alone must never determine whether the user can access every record in the system.
[2887] RBAC must therefore be combined with data scope and contextual rules.
[2889] 4.12 Role Structure
[2890] The platform should support:
[2891] System roles
[2892] Examples:
[2893] Super Administrator
[2894] Security Administrator
[2895] Identity Administrator
[2896] Audit Administrator
[2897] Business roles
[2898] Examples:
[2899] Claims Officer
[2900] Claims Supervisor
[2901] Medical Officer
[2902] Finance Officer
[2903] Finance Manager
[2904] Provider Manager
[2905] Customer Service Officer
[2906] Employer Account Manager
[2907] Compliance Officer
[2908] Executive roles
[2909] Examples:
[2910] CFO
[2911] COO
[2912] Medical Director
[2913] CEO
[2914] External roles
[2915] Examples:
[2916] Employer Administrator
[2917] Provider Administrator
[2918] Provider Claims Officer
[2919] Member
[2921] 4.13 Role Hierarchy
[2922] The system may support controlled role inheritance.
[2923] Example:
[2924] Claims Manager
[2925] ↓
[2926] inherits
[2927] ↓
[2928] Claims Supervisor
[2929] ↓
[2930] inherits
[2931] ↓
[2932] Claims Officer
[2933] However, inheritance must not automatically create unlimited privileges.
[2934] Every inherited permission must be visible and auditable.
[2936] 4.14 Permission Model
[2937] Permissions shall follow a structured naming convention.
[2938] Recommended format:
[2939] <domain>.<resource>.<action>
[2940] Examples:
[2941] member.member.read
[2942] member.member.create
[2943] member.member.update
[2945] claims.claim.read
[2946] claims.claim.create
[2947] claims.claim.approve
[2948] claims.claim.reject
[2950] finance.payment.read
[2951] finance.payment.create
[2952] finance.payment.approve
[2953] finance.payment.execute
[2955] provider.provider.read
[2956] provider.provider.create
[2957] provider.provider.approve
[2959] 4.15 Permission Actions
[2960] Common actions include:
[2961] READ
[2962] CREATE
[2963] UPDATE
[2964] DELETE
[2965] SUBMIT
[2966] VERIFY
[2967] REVIEW
[2968] APPROVE
[2969] REJECT
[2970] CANCEL
[2971] REVERSE
[2972] EXPORT
[2973] DOWNLOAD
[2974] PRINT
[2975] ASSIGN
[2976] ESCALATE
[2977] EXECUTE
[2978] CONFIGURE
[2979] ADMINISTER
[2980] Deletion should be restricted heavily for business-critical records.
[2981] In many cases the correct action is:
[2982] ARCHIVE
[2983] DEACTIVATE
[2984] CANCEL
[2985] REVERSE
[2986] rather than physical deletion.
[2988] 4.16 Core Permission Domains
[2989] The permission system should cover at least:
[2990] identity
[2991] organization
[2992] member
[2993] dependant
[2994] employer
[2995] enrollment
[2996] plan
[2997] provider
[2998] credentialing
[2999] eligibility
[3000] preauthorization
[3001] referral
[3002] claims
[3003] medical_review
[3004] utilization
[3005] pharmacy
[3006] laboratory
[3007] complaints
[3008] customer_service
[3009] finance
[3010] billing
[3011] payments
[3012] settlement
[3013] reconciliation
[3014] reporting
[3015] analytics
[3016] documents
[3017] notifications
[3018] audit
[3019] compliance
[3020] security
[3021] system_configuration
[3022] integration
[3024] 4.17 Example Role-Permission Matrix
[3025] * subject to approval thresholds and SoD.
[3026] ** User administration must not automatically grant business privileges.
[3028] 4.18 RBAC Must Not Equal Data Access
[3029] Example:
[3030] A Claims Officer may have:
[3031] claims.claim.read
[3032] But that does not mean:
[3033] ALL CLAIMS
[3034] The user may instead have:
[3035] Region = Lagos
[3036] Department = Claims
[3037] Claim Type = Standard
[3038] Therefore:
[3039] Permission + Scope = Effective Access
[3041] 4.19 Scope-Based Access Control
[3042] Scopes determine which records a user can access.
[3043] Possible scopes:
[3044] GLOBAL
[3045] ORGANIZATION
[3046] REGION
[3047] BRANCH
[3048] DEPARTMENT
[3049] TEAM
[3050] EMPLOYER
[3051] PROVIDER
[3052] MEMBER
[3053] CLAIM
[3054] PLAN
[3055] COST_CENTER
[3056] FINANCIAL_ACCOUNT
[3057] Example:
[3058] Role:
[3059] Claims Officer
[3061] Permission:
[3062] claims.claim.read
[3064] Scope:
[3065] Branch = Lagos Island
[3067] Result:
[3068] Can read claims belonging to authorized Lagos Island operations.
[3070] 4.20 Scope Hierarchy
[3071] A configurable organizational hierarchy may be:
[3072] HMO
[3073] ├── Region
[3074] │    ├── Branch
[3075] │    │    ├── Department
[3076] │    │    │    └── Team
[3077] │
[3078] ├── Provider Network
[3079] │
[3080] └── Corporate Employers
[3081] The authorization engine must understand inherited organizational scope.
[3083] 4.21 Employer Tenant Isolation
[3084] Employer users must only access their organization's information.
[3085] Example:
[3086] Employer A
[3087] ↓
[3088] Employees
[3089] Members
[3090] Enrollment
[3091] Invoices
[3092] Reports
[3093] Employer A must never retrieve:
[3094] Employer B's employees
[3095] Employer B's invoices
[3096] Employer B's claims
[3097] Employer B's reports
[3098] This restriction must be enforced server-side.
[3100] 4.22 Provider Tenant Isolation
[3101] Provider users must similarly be restricted.
[3102] A provider user should only access records associated with the authorized provider organization.
[3103] Example:
[3104] Provider ABC
[3105] ↓
[3106] Provider users
[3107] ↓
[3108] Provider claims
[3109] Provider authorizations
[3110] Provider settlements
[3111] Provider documents
[3112] Cross-provider access requires explicit authorization.
[3114] 4.23 ABAC Architecture
[3115] ABAC evaluates attributes in addition to roles.
[3116] Example policy:
[3117] ALLOW claim.approve
[3118] IF
[3119] user.role = ClaimsManager
[3120] AND
[3121] claim.amount <= approval_limit
[3122] AND
[3123] claim.organization = user.organization
[3124] AND
[3125] claim.status = APPROVAL_REVIEW
[3126] AND
[3127] claim.created_by != user.id
[3128] AND
[3129] user.mfa_verified = true
[3130] This is considerably safer than:
[3131] IF role = ClaimsManager
[3132] THEN approve claim
[3134] 4.24 User Attributes
[3135] Authorization may evaluate:
[3136] User role
[3137] Department
[3138] Branch
[3139] Region
[3140] Job level
[3141] Employment status
[3142] Organization
[3143] Manager
[3144] Clearance level
[3145] MFA status
[3146] Device trust
[3147] Session risk
[3148] Training status
[3149] Temporary assignment
[3150] Privileged-access status
[3152] 4.25 Resource Attributes
[3153] Resources may have:
[3154] Organization
[3155] Branch
[3156] Department
[3157] Owner
[3158] Sensitivity classification
[3159] Financial value
[3160] Medical sensitivity
[3161] Provider
[3162] Employer
[3163] Member
[3164] Claim type
[3165] Status
[3166] Risk score
[3167] Approval state
[3169] 4.26 Environmental Attributes
[3170] The authorization engine may evaluate:
[3171] Time
[3172] Location context
[3173] Device trust
[3174] Network context
[3175] Session age
[3176] Authentication strength
[3177] Risk score
[3178] Emergency state
[3179] Maintenance mode
[3180] Example:
[3181] Export sensitive medical data
[3182] AND
[3183] untrusted device
[3184] → DENY
[3186] 4.27 Sensitive Data Classification
[3187] Data should be classified according to sensitivity.
[3188] Example:
[3189] Level 1 — Public
[3190] Public provider information
[3191] Public company information
[3192] Level 2 — Internal
[3193] Operational reports
[3194] Internal configuration
[3195] Level 3 — Confidential
[3196] Employer information
[3197] Financial records
[3198] Internal claims data
[3199] Level 4 — Highly Sensitive
[3200] Medical information
[3201] Identification documents
[3202] Financial account details
[3203] Security credentials
[3204] Sensitive audit information
[3205] Higher classification requires stronger controls.
[3207] 4.28 Field-Level Access Control
[3208] Two users may access the same record but see different fields.
[3209] Example:
[3210] Customer Service
[3211] May see:
[3212] Member Name
[3213] Member ID
[3214] Plan
[3215] Coverage Status
[3216] Contact Information
[3217] Finance
[3218] May see:
[3219] Billing information
[3220] Payment information
[3221] Account references
[3222] Medical Officer
[3223] May access appropriate clinical information.
[3224] System Administrator
[3225] May technically manage the system but should not automatically receive unrestricted medical or financial visibility.
[3227] 4.29 Data Masking
[3228] Sensitive fields should support masking.
[3229] Example:
[3230] Bank Account:
[3231] ********4321
[3233] National Identifier:
[3234] ********1234
[3235] Unmasking should require:
[3236] Explicit permission
[3237] Appropriate business purpose
[3238] Possibly step-up authentication
[3239] Audit event
[3241] 4.30 Segregation of Duties
[3242] The system must prevent dangerous combinations of privileges.
[3243] Examples:
[3244] Claim Creator
[3245] ≠
[3246] Claim Approver
[3247] Payment Creator
[3248] ≠
[3249] Payment Approver
[3250] Payment Approver
[3251] ≠
[3252] Payment Executor
[3253] Provider Bank Account Modifier
[3254] ≠
[3255] Provider Settlement Approver
[3256] User Privilege Creator
[3257] ≠
[3258] Privilege Approver
[3260] 4.31 SoD Policy Examples
[3261] The platform should support configurable conflict rules.
[3262] Example:
[3263] CONFLICT-001
[3265] Permission A:
[3266] claims.claim.create
[3268] Permission B:
[3269] claims.claim.approve
[3271] Rule:
[3272] Same user cannot possess both within same authorization scope.
[3273] Another:
[3274] CONFLICT-002
[3276] Permission A:
[3277] finance.payment.create
[3279] Permission B:
[3280] finance.payment.execute
[3282] 4.32 Approval Engine
[3283] The platform shall have a centralized approval engine.
[3284] Approval decisions may depend on:
[3285] Transaction Type
[3286] Amount
[3287] Risk
[3288] Department
[3289] Role
[3290] Organization
[3291] Provider
[3292] Employer
[3293] Claim Type
[3294] Exception Status
[3295] Previous Approvals
[3296] User Relationship
[3297] SoD
[3298] Example:
[3299] Payment
[3300] ₦2,500,000
[3301] ↓
[3302] Approval Rule
[3303] ↓
[3304] Finance Manager
[3305] ↓
[3306] Controller
[3307] ↓
[3308] Execution
[3310] 4.33 Approval Escalation
[3311] If an approver does not act within a configured period:
[3312] Pending
[3313] ↓
[3314] Reminder
[3315] ↓
[3316] Escalation
[3317] ↓
[3318] Secondary Approver
[3319] ↓
[3320] Management Escalation
[3321] The system must prevent unauthorized users from manually bypassing escalation.
[3323] 4.34 Self-Approval Prevention
[3324] The platform must detect relationships such as:
[3325] created_by = current_user
[3326] and prevent prohibited approval.
[3327] It should also detect indirect conflicts where configured.
[3328] Example:
[3329] User creates payment
[3330] ↓
[3331] Attempts approval
[3332] ↓
[3333] SoD Engine
[3334] ↓
[3335] DENY
[3337] 4.35 Privileged Access Management
[3338] Privileged users should receive additional controls.
[3339] Examples:
[3340] System administrators
[3341] Security administrators
[3342] Identity administrators
[3343] Database administrators
[3344] Infrastructure administrators
[3345] Privileged access should support:
[3346] MFA
[3347] Time-limited elevation
[3348] Approval
[3349] Session logging
[3350] Activity monitoring
[3351] Automatic expiration
[3352] Emergency revocation
[3354] 4.36 Just-In-Time Privileges
[3355] Instead of permanently assigning dangerous privileges:
[3356] Normal user
[3357] ↓
[3358] Requests elevated access
[3359] ↓
[3360] Manager/Security approval
[3361] ↓
[3362] Privilege activated
[3363] ↓
[3364] 30 minutes / 2 hours / configured duration
[3365] ↓
[3366] Automatic expiration
[3368] 4.37 Temporary Access
[3369] Temporary privileges must contain:
[3370] Access ID
[3371] User
[3372] Role/Permission
[3373] Scope
[3374] Reason
[3375] Approver
[3376] Start Time
[3377] Expiration Time
[3378] Ticket/Request
[3379] Created By
[3380] The system must automatically revoke expired temporary privileges.
[3382] 4.38 Emergency / Break-Glass Access
[3383] Emergency access may be required for critical operational situations.
[3384] Example:
[3385] Critical incident
[3386] ↓
[3387] Authorized user activates emergency access
[3388] ↓
[3389] Reason required
[3390] ↓
[3391] Additional authentication
[3392] ↓
[3393] Temporary elevated access
[3394] ↓
[3395] Enhanced audit logging
[3396] ↓
[3397] Automatic expiry
[3398] ↓
[3399] Mandatory post-incident review
[3400] Break-glass must never become a hidden permanent administrator mechanism.
[3402] 4.39 Access Request Workflow
[3403] User Requests Access
[3404] ↓
[3405] Business Justification
[3406] ↓
[3407] Manager Review
[3408] ↓
[3409] Data Owner Review
[3410] ↓
[3411] Security/Compliance Review
[3412] ↓
[3413] Approval
[3414] ↓
[3415] Provisioning
[3416] ↓
[3417] Audit
[3418] High-risk access may require multiple approvals.
[3420] 4.40 Access Revocation
[3421] Access must be revoked when:
[3422] Employee leaves
[3423] Employee is suspended
[3424] Role changes
[3425] Department changes
[3426] Branch changes
[3427] Contract expires
[3428] Provider relationship ends
[3429] Employer relationship ends
[3430] Temporary access expires
[3431] Security incident occurs
[3432] Device is compromised
[3433] Critical revocation should happen immediately.
[3435] 4.41 Joiner-Mover-Leaver Process
[3436] Joiner
[3437] Employee created
[3438] → Identity created
[3439] → Role assigned
[3440] → Scope assigned
[3441] → MFA enrolled
[3442] → Access activated
[3443] Mover
[3444] Department/Role changes
[3445] → Existing access reviewed
[3446] → Old privileges removed
[3447] → New privileges evaluated
[3448] → New access approved
[3449] → Audit recorded
[3450] Leaver
[3451] Termination
[3452] → Account disabled
[3453] → Sessions revoked
[3454] → Tokens invalidated
[3455] → Privileges removed
[3456] → API credentials revoked
[3457] → Devices disconnected
[3458] → Audit recorded
[3460] 4.42 Access Recertification
[3461] Managers should periodically review user access.
[3462] Example:
[3463] Monthly:
[3464] Privileged access
[3466] Quarterly:
[3467] Sensitive business permissions
[3469] Semi-annually:
[3470] Standard employee access
[3471] The exact schedule should be configurable.
[3472] Review outcomes:
[3473] KEEP
[3474] MODIFY
[3475] REVOKE
[3476] ESCALATE
[3478] 4.43 Dormant Accounts
[3479] The platform should detect accounts with prolonged inactivity.
[3480] Possible lifecycle:
[3481] Active
[3482] ↓
[3483] Dormant Warning
[3484] ↓
[3485] Restricted
[3486] ↓
[3487] Suspended
[3488] ↓
[3489] Deactivated
[3490] Before deactivation, appropriate administrators should receive notifications.
[3492] 4.44 Service-to-Service Authorization
[3493] Microservices must authenticate and authorize each other.
[3494] Example:
[3495] Claims Service
[3496] ↓
[3497] Authorization Service
[3498] A service should not automatically trust another service merely because it is inside the private network.
[3499] Service authorization should consider:
[3500] Service identity
[3501] Requested operation
[3502] Resource
[3503] Scope
[3504] Environment
[3505] Token
[3506] Certificate where applicable
[3507] Policy
[3509] 4.45 Service Accounts
[3510] Every service account should have:
[3511] Service ID
[3512] Owner
[3513] Purpose
[3514] Permissions
[3515] Scope
[3516] Environment
[3517] Created Date
[3518] Expiration
[3519] Last Used
[3520] Credential Status
[3521] Unused service credentials must be detected and removed.
[3523] 4.46 API Authorization
[3524] Every API endpoint must enforce authorization.
[3525] Example:
[3526] GET /members/{id}
[3527] must validate:
[3528] Authenticated?
[3529] +
[3530] member.read permission?
[3531] +
[3532] correct organization?
[3533] +
[3534] correct scope?
[3535] +
[3536] member accessible?
[3537] Never rely solely on:
[3538] The user is logged in.
[3540] 4.47 IDOR Protection
[3541] The platform must prevent insecure direct object reference attacks.
[3542] Example attack:
[3543] GET /claims/CLAIM-1001
[3544] changing the ID to:
[3545] GET /claims/CLAIM-1002
[3546] must not expose another organization's claim.
[3547] Authorization must occur against the actual resource.
[3549] 4.48 Mass Assignment Protection
[3550] APIs must not blindly accept privileged fields.
[3551] Unsafe example:
[3552] {
[3553] "role": "SUPER_ADMIN",
[3554] "status": "ACTIVE"
[3555] }
[3556] The backend must explicitly define which fields the caller is permitted to modify.
[3558] 4.49 Export and Download Authorization
[3559] Export permissions require additional controls.
[3560] Examples:
[3561] member.export
[3562] claims.export
[3563] finance.export
[3564] medical.export
[3565] audit.export
[3566] Export authorization should evaluate:
[3567] Role
[3568] Scope
[3569] Dataset size
[3570] Data sensitivity
[3571] Purpose
[3572] User risk
[3573] MFA
[3574] Approval requirements
[3575] Large exports should be monitored and potentially require approval.
[3577] 4.50 Bulk Operation Controls
[3578] Bulk operations are high risk.
[3579] Examples:
[3580] Bulk member update
[3581] Bulk enrollment
[3582] Bulk claim import
[3583] Bulk payment
[3584] Bulk user creation
[3585] Bulk privilege assignment
[3586] Controls should include:
[3587] Permission
[3588] +
[3589] Scope
[3590] +
[3591] Validation
[3592] +
[3593] Preview
[3594] +
[3595] Approval
[3596] +
[3597] Execution
[3598] +
[3599] Audit
[3601] 4.51 Authorization Decision Engine
[3602] The authorization engine should produce a structured decision.
[3603] Example:
[3604] {
[3605] "decision": "DENY",
[3606] "reason": "INSUFFICIENT_SCOPE",
[3607] "userId": "USR-1001",
[3608] "resource": "CLAIM-20091",
[3609] "action": "APPROVE",
[3610] "policy": "CLAIMS-APPROVAL-004",
[3611] "correlationId": "COR-883920"
[3612] }
[3613] Users should receive safe error messages without exposing internal security logic.
[3615] 4.52 Authorization Caching
[3616] Authorization decisions may be cached for performance.
[3617] However, cache invalidation must be carefully designed.
[3618] Example:
[3619] Role revoked
[3620] ↓
[3621] Permission cache invalidated
[3622] ↓
[3623] Sessions/tokens re-evaluated
[3624] ↓
[3625] Access immediately restricted
[3626] Security-sensitive changes should not wait for a long cache expiration period.
[3628] 4.53 Policy Versioning
[3629] Authorization policies must be version controlled.
[3630] Example:
[3631] Policy:
[3632] CLAIMS-APPROVAL
[3634] Version:
[3635] 3.2
[3637] Status:
[3638] ACTIVE
[3639] Historical authorization events should record the policy version used.
[3641] 4.54 Authorization Audit Events
[3642] Sensitive events should capture:
[3643] Event ID
[3644] User ID
[3645] Organization ID
[3646] Action
[3647] Resource
[3648] Resource ID
[3649] Permission
[3650] Scope
[3651] Decision
[3652] Reason
[3653] Policy ID
[3654] Policy Version
[3655] IP/Device Context where appropriate
[3656] Timestamp
[3657] Correlation ID
[3658] Examples:
[3659] ACCESS_GRANTED
[3660] ACCESS_DENIED
[3661] PRIVILEGE_GRANTED
[3662] PRIVILEGE_REVOKED
[3663] ROLE_CHANGED
[3664] MFA_CHANGED
[3665] SESSION_REVOKED
[3666] BREAK_GLASS_USED
[3667] EXPORT_EXECUTED
[3669] 4.55 Security Monitoring
[3670] The security system should detect patterns such as:
[3671] Privilege escalation
[3672] User receives unusual administrator privileges
[3673] Access anomaly
[3674] User suddenly accesses thousands of records
[3675] Cross-tenant attempt
[3676] Employer A repeatedly attempts Employer B records
[3677] Credential anomaly
[3678] Same account appears on multiple suspicious devices
[3679] Export anomaly
[3680] User downloads unusually large datasets
[3682] 4.56 Risk-Based Authorization
[3683] Authorization can incorporate risk.
[3684] Example:
[3685] Normal request
[3686] → Allow
[3688] Unusual device
[3689] → MFA
[3691] High-risk action
[3692] → Step-up authentication
[3694] Extremely suspicious action
[3695] → Deny + Security Alert
[3696] Risk must not silently override mandatory authorization requirements.
[3698] 4.57 Authorization for Background Jobs
[3699] Automated jobs must have explicit identities.
[3700] Example:
[3701] Daily Billing Job
[3702] should have:
[3703] billing.invoice.read
[3704] billing.invoice.generate
[3705] but not:
[3706] finance.payment.execute
[3707] unless explicitly required.
[3708] Scheduled processes must follow the same least-privilege principle as human users.
[3710] 4.58 Authorization for Integrations
[3711] External integrations should receive dedicated credentials and scopes.
[3712] Example:
[3713] Payment Gateway
[3714] → payment.status.read
[3715] → payment.webhook.write
[3716] It should not receive:
[3717] member.delete
[3718] finance.configure
[3719] user.admin
[3721] 4.59 API Credential Lifecycle
[3722] API credentials should support:
[3723] CREATED
[3724] ACTIVE
[3725] ROTATION_REQUIRED
[3726] EXPIRED
[3727] REVOKED
[3728] COMPROMISED
[3729] Credential rotation must not require unnecessary service downtime.
[3731] 4.60 Critical Access-Control Edge Cases
[3732] The platform must explicitly handle:
[3734] 4.61 Unknown Edge-Case Strategy
[3735] The system cannot realistically predict every future failure.
[3736] Therefore, the architecture must make unknown conditions safe by design.
[3737] When an unexpected authorization condition occurs:
[3738] Unexpected State
[3739] ↓
[3740] DENY OR SAFE RESTRICTION
[3741] ↓
[3742] Preserve Transaction State
[3743] ↓
[3744] Generate Correlation ID
[3745] ↓
[3746] Log Technical Error
[3747] ↓
[3748] Create Security Event Where Appropriate
[3749] ↓
[3750] Notify Monitoring
[3751] ↓
[3752] Do Not Silently Continue
[3753] ↓
[3754] Controlled Recovery
[3755] The most important rule is:
[3756] An unknown authorization condition must never accidentally become an authorization grant.
[3758] 4.62 Last-Administrator Protection
[3759] The platform must prevent accidental removal of all administrative capability.
[3760] Example:
[3761] Admin A attempts to remove Admin B
[3762] If Admin B is the only remaining authorized security administrator:
[3763] DENY
[3764] unless an approved replacement administrator exists.
[3766] 4.63 Concurrent Authorization Changes
[3767] Example:
[3768] User A has approval permission
