[10904] hmo_amount
[10905] rejected_amount
[10906] decision
[10907] reason_code
[10909] 9.25 ADJUDICATION DATA
[10910] Adjudication must be stored separately from the claim itself.
[10911] Example:
[10912] adjudication_id
[10913] claim_id
[10914] rule_set_version
[10915] decision
[10916] decision_reason
[10917] calculated_amount
[10918] review_status
[10919] reviewer_id
[10920] approved_by
[10921] created_at
[10922] This allows the platform to reproduce how the decision was reached.
[10924] 9.26 RULE VERSIONING
[10925] Every material automated decision should be associated with the applicable rule version.
[10926] Examples:
[10927] Benefit Rule Version
[10928] Tariff Rule Version
[10929] Eligibility Rule Version
[10930] Adjudication Rule Version
[10931] Risk Rule Version
[10932] Approval Rule Version
[10933] If a rule changes tomorrow, yesterday's claim must still be explainable using yesterday's rule.
[10935] 9.27 FINANCIAL DATA
[10936] Financial records include:
[10937] Invoice
[10938] Invoice line
[10939] Payment
[10940] Payment allocation
[10941] Settlement
[10942] Settlement line
[10943] Refund
[10944] Adjustment
[10945] Credit note
[10946] Debit note
[10947] Ledger transaction
[10948] Reconciliation record
[10949] Financial records should be immutable after finalization except through controlled correction/reversal mechanisms.
[10951] 9.28 DOUBLE-ENTRY LEDGER
[10952] Financial transactions should use double-entry accounting.
[10953] Example:
[10954] Debit:
[10955] Provider Claims Expense
[10957] Credit:
[10958] Provider Payable
[10959] Every financial transaction must balance.
[10960] Conceptually:
[10961] TOTAL DEBITS = TOTAL CREDITS
[10962] The ledger should not depend on manually maintained balances alone.
[10964] 9.29 CUSTOMER CASE DATA
[10965] Case structure:
[10966] case_id
[10967] case_number
[10968] customer_type
[10969] customer_id
[10970] category
[10971] subcategory
[10972] priority
[10973] status
[10974] assigned_team
[10975] assigned_user
[10976] sla_id
[10977] opened_at
[10978] resolved_at
[10979] closed_at
[10980] created_at
[10981] updated_at
[10982] Related business objects should be linked rather than duplicated unnecessarily.
[10984] 9.30 COMPLAINT DATA
[10985] Complaints should contain:
[10986] complaint_id
[10987] complaint_number
[10988] customer_id
[10989] category
[10990] priority
[10991] status
[10992] assigned_team
[10993] investigation_status
[10994] resolution
[10995] opened_at
[10996] resolved_at
[10997] closed_at
[10999] 9.31 DOCUMENT DATA MODEL
[11000] Documents should have metadata separate from the physical file.
[11001] document_id
[11002] document_type
[11003] entity_type
[11004] entity_id
[11005] classification
[11006] file_name
[11007] file_hash
[11008] storage_reference
[11009] mime_type
[11010] size
[11011] scan_status
[11012] version
[11013] uploaded_by
[11014] uploaded_at
[11015] status
[11016] This allows documents to be attached to multiple business domains without embedding files directly in transactional records.
[11018] 9.32 DOCUMENT VERSIONING
[11019] A document should support:
[11020] Version 1
[11021] Version 2
[11022] Version 3
[11023] The system must preserve previous versions where retention rules require them.
[11024] A new version must not silently replace historical evidence.
[11026] 9.33 IDENTITY DATA
[11027] Identity data should include:
[11028] user_id
[11029] username
[11030] email
[11031] phone
[11032] status
[11033] identity_type
[11034] organization_id
[11035] employee_id
[11036] member_id
[11037] provider_user_id
[11038] last_login
[11039] created_at
[11040] updated_at
[11041] Authentication credentials should be separated from general profile information.
[11043] 9.34 ROLE AND PERMISSION DATA
[11044] Core entities:
[11045] role
[11046] permission
[11047] role_permission
[11048] user_role
[11049] policy
[11050] policy_rule
[11051] scope
[11052] user_scope
[11053] A user's effective access is calculated from these structures plus contextual rules.
[11055] 9.35 AUDIT DATA
[11056] Audit events should contain:
[11057] audit_id
[11058] actor_id
[11059] actor_type
[11060] organization_id
[11061] action
[11062] resource_type
[11063] resource_id
[11064] previous_state
[11065] new_state
[11066] timestamp
[11067] ip_address
[11068] device_id
[11069] session_id
[11070] correlation_id
[11071] reason
[11072] policy_version
[11073] Audit records should be append-only.
[11075] 9.36 SECURITY EVENT DATA
[11076] Security events should be distinct from ordinary business audit events.
[11077] Examples:
[11078] LOGIN_FAILURE
[11079] MFA_FAILURE
[11080] PRIVILEGE_CHANGE
[11081] UNAUTHORIZED_ACCESS
[11082] CROSS_TENANT_ATTEMPT
[11083] BULK_EXPORT
[11084] SUSPICIOUS_SESSION
[11085] POLICY_VIOLATION
[11086] Security events may require different retention, monitoring, and alerting.
[11088] 9.37 MASTER DATA MANAGEMENT
[11089] Master Data Management ensures important entities have one authoritative identity.
[11090] Master entities include:
[11091] Member
[11092] Employer
[11093] Provider
[11094] Plan
[11095] Service
[11096] Organization
[11097] User
[11098] The system should prevent multiple records representing the same real-world entity unless explicitly justified.
[11100] 9.38 DUPLICATE DETECTION
[11101] Possible matching fields:
[11102] Member
[11103] Name
[11104] Date of birth
[11105] Phone
[11106] Email
[11107] Identification reference
[11108] Employer reference
[11109] Provider
[11110] Legal name
[11111] Registration reference
[11112] Address
[11113] Phone
[11114] Accreditation information
[11115] Duplicate detection should produce confidence levels rather than relying only on exact matches.
[11117] 9.39 MASTER DATA MERGE
[11118] If two records are confirmed duplicates:
[11119] Record A
[11120] Record B
[11121] ↓
[11122] Duplicate Review
[11123] ↓
[11124] Merge Approval
[11125] ↓
[11126] Master Record
[11127] The merge must preserve historical references.
[11128] Old identifiers should resolve to the surviving master record.
[11130] 9.40 MASTER DATA SPLIT
[11131] A merge may sometimes be incorrect.
[11132] The platform should support controlled splitting where appropriate.
[11133] The split must:
[11134] Preserve audit history
[11135] Preserve financial history
[11136] Preserve clinical history
[11137] Correct relationships
[11138] Require appropriate authorization
[11139] Record reason
[11141] 9.41 DATA QUALITY FRAMEWORK
[11142] Data quality should be measured across:
[11143] Accuracy
[11144] Is the information correct?
[11145] Completeness
[11146] Are required fields populated?
[11147] Consistency
[11148] Does the same entity have conflicting information?
[11149] Timeliness
[11150] Is the information current?
[11151] Uniqueness
[11152] Are duplicate records present?
[11153] Validity
[11154] Does the data follow defined rules?
[11156] 9.42 DATA QUALITY RULE ENGINE
[11157] Examples:
[11158] Member must have valid membership identifier.
[11160] Provider must have valid status.
[11162] Plan version cannot overlap another active version.
[11164] Claim must reference existing member.
[11166] Claim service date cannot be invalid.
[11168] Payment cannot exceed approved amount without authorized adjustment.
[11169] Failed rules should produce actionable data-quality events.
[11171] 9.43 DATA OWNERSHIP
[11172] Every major dataset should have an owner.
[11173] Example:
[11174] Ownership determines who may approve structural or business changes.
[11176] 9.44 DATA STEWARDSHIP
[11177] Data stewards are responsible for:
[11178] Quality
[11179] Corrections
[11180] Duplicate resolution
[11181] Validation rules
[11182] Data standards
[11183] Exception management
[11184] A data steward should not automatically receive unrestricted access to every sensitive dataset.
[11186] 9.45 DATA CLASSIFICATION
[11187] Suggested classification:
[11188] PUBLIC
[11189] INTERNAL
[11190] CONFIDENTIAL
[11191] RESTRICTED
[11192] HIGHLY_RESTRICTED
[11193] Examples:
[11194] Public
[11195] Provider directory information approved for public use.
[11196] Internal
[11197] Operational procedures.
[11198] Confidential
[11199] Employer and customer contact information.
[11200] Restricted
[11201] Healthcare and financial information.
[11202] Highly Restricted
[11203] Security credentials, investigation records, sensitive clinical information, privileged security data.
[11205] 9.46 FIELD-LEVEL PROTECTION
[11206] Sensitive fields should support:
[11207] Masking
[11208] Encryption
[11209] Tokenization where appropriate
[11210] Access logging
[11211] Restricted search
[11212] Restricted export
[11213] Example:
[11214] Phone:
[11215] 0803****456
[11217] Bank Account:
[11218] ********1234
[11220] 9.47 ENCRYPTION
[11221] Sensitive data should be encrypted:
[11222] At Rest
[11223] Use strong modern encryption.
[11224] In Transit
[11225] Use secure transport protocols.
[11226] Application Secrets
[11227] Store in a dedicated secrets-management system.
[11228] Encryption keys should be managed separately from application data.
[11230] 9.48 DATA RETENTION
[11231] Retention policies should be configurable by data class.
[11232] Examples:
[11233] Operational data
[11234] Financial records
[11235] Claims
[11236] Clinical information
[11237] Audit records
[11238] Security events
[11239] Customer cases
[11240] Documents
[11241] Retention should consider:
[11242] Regulatory requirements
[11243] Legal obligations
[11244] Contractual requirements
[11245] Business needs
[11246] Litigation/legal holds
[11248] 9.49 LEGAL HOLD
[11249] When a record is subject to legal or compliance hold:
[11250] Retention Expiry
[11251] ↓
[11252] LEGAL HOLD
[11253] ↓
[11254] Automatic Destruction Blocked
[11255] The hold must be auditable.
[11257] 9.50 DATA DELETION
[11258] Deletion must distinguish:
[11259] Soft Delete
[11260] Record becomes inactive but remains available for controlled historical access.
[11261] Hard Delete
[11262] Physical destruction.
[11263] Hard deletion should be highly restricted and should not be permitted where it would violate:
[11264] Financial integrity
[11265] Audit requirements
[11266] Legal holds
[11267] Historical healthcare integrity
[11268] Regulatory retention
[11270] 9.51 DATA CORRECTION
[11271] A correction should preserve:
[11272] Original Value
[11273] New Value
[11274] Reason
[11275] Actor
[11276] Timestamp
[11277] Approval
[11278] Evidence
[11279] For sensitive information, corrections may require additional verification.
[11281] 9.52 DATA IMPORT ARCHITECTURE
[11282] Imports should follow:
[11283] UPLOAD
[11284] ↓
[11285] MALWARE SCAN
[11286] ↓
[11287] FILE VALIDATION
[11288] ↓
[11289] SCHEMA VALIDATION
[11290] ↓
[11291] FIELD VALIDATION
[11292] ↓
[11293] BUSINESS VALIDATION
[11294] ↓
[11295] DUPLICATE DETECTION
[11296] ↓
[11297] PREVIEW
[11298] ↓
[11299] APPROVAL
[11300] ↓
[11301] PROCESSING
[11302] ↓
[11303] RESULT REPORT
[11304] Large imports should be asynchronous.
[11306] 9.53 DATA EXPORT ARCHITECTURE
[11307] Exports should require:
[11308] Permission
[11309] Scope validation
[11310] Purpose/reason where required
[11311] Sensitive-data controls
[11312] Record-count limits
[11313] Audit logging
[11314] Expiring download links
[11315] Exports should be generated asynchronously for large datasets.
[11317] 9.54 CROSS-TENANT DATA ISOLATION
[11318] This is a critical requirement.
[11319] An employer must never access:
[11320] Another Employer's Members
[11321] A provider must never access:
[11322] Another Provider's Claims
[11323] An HMO regional employee must not automatically access:
[11324] Another Region
[11325] unless their scope explicitly allows it.
[11326] Isolation must exist at:
[11327] API
[11328] Service
[11329] Query
[11330] Database
[11331] Cache
[11332] Search
[11333] Export
[11334] Reporting
[11335] levels.
[11337] 9.55 CACHE SECURITY
[11338] Sensitive data must not be cached without appropriate controls.
[11339] Cache keys must include tenant/scope context where required.
[11340] Example:
[11341] CACHE:
[11342] provider:{provider_id}:eligibility:{member_id}
[11343] should not be reused across organizations without verifying authorization context.
[11345] 9.56 SEARCH INDEX SECURITY
[11346] Search systems can accidentally become a data-leak channel.
[11347] Therefore:
[11348] Search authorization must be enforced before records are returned.
[11349] Search indexes should respect:
[11350] Organization
[11351] Provider
[11352] Employer
[11353] Region
[11354] Department
[11355] Role
[11356] Data classification
[11358] 9.57 DATABASE TRANSACTION INTEGRITY
[11359] Critical operations should be atomic.
[11360] Example claim approval:
[11361] Claim Approval
[11362] +
[11363] Adjudication Result
[11364] +
[11365] Approval Record
[11366] +
[11367] Audit Event
[11368] must not leave the system in an impossible partial state.
[11369] Where distributed transactions are unavoidable, use appropriate patterns such as:
[11370] Saga
[11371] Transactional outbox
[11372] Idempotent consumers
[11373] Compensating actions
[11375] 9.58 EVENT DATA
[11376] Domain events may include:
[11377] MemberEnrolled
[11378] MemberSuspended
[11379] ProviderApproved
[11380] PlanActivated
[11381] AuthorizationApproved
[11382] ClaimSubmitted
[11383] ClaimApproved
[11384] ClaimPaid
[11385] PaymentReconciled
[11386] ComplaintOpened
[11387] ComplaintResolved
[11388] UserRoleChanged
[11389] Each event should contain:
[11390] event_id
[11391] event_type
[11392] aggregate_type
[11393] aggregate_id
[11394] event_version
[11395] timestamp
[11396] producer
[11397] correlation_id
[11398] causation_id
[11399] payload
[11401] 9.59 EVENT VERSIONING
[11402] Events may evolve.
[11403] Example:
[11404] ClaimApproved.v1
[11405] ClaimApproved.v2
[11406] Consumers should not break simply because the producer adds new fields.
[11407] Backward compatibility should be considered.
[11409] 9.60 DATA CONSISTENCY MODEL
[11410] The platform should distinguish between:
[11411] Strong Consistency
[11412] Use for:
[11413] Financial transactions
[11414] Claim approval
[11415] Payment
[11416] Permission changes
[11417] Critical eligibility state
[11418] Eventual Consistency
[11419] May be appropriate for:
[11420] Analytics
[11421] Dashboards
[11422] Search indexes
[11423] Notification delivery
[11424] Non-critical reporting
[11425] The choice must be explicit.
[11427] 9.61 DATA CONCURRENCY
[11428] Important records should support optimistic concurrency.
[11429] Example:
[11430] Record Version = 10
[11431] Update requires:
[11432] Expected Version = 10
[11433] If current version is:
[11434] 11
[11435] the update fails safely.
[11436] This prevents stale updates.
[11438] 9.62 BACKUP ARCHITECTURE
[11439] Backups should include:
[11440] Transactional database
[11441] Configuration
[11442] Critical object storage
[11443] Audit data
[11444] Required system metadata
[11445] Backups must be:
[11446] Encrypted
[11447] Monitored
[11448] Tested
[11449] Access-controlled
[11450] Versioned
[11451] A backup that has never been restored successfully should not be assumed reliable.
[11453] 9.63 DISASTER RECOVERY
[11454] The platform should define:
[11455] RPO
[11456] Maximum acceptable data loss.
[11457] RTO
[11458] Maximum acceptable recovery time.
[11459] Targets should be defined per service rather than assuming every module has identical requirements.
[11460] Example classification:
[11461] Tier 1:
[11462] Identity
[11463] Eligibility
[11464] Claims
[11465] Finance
[11467] Tier 2:
[11468] Customer support
[11469] Provider management
[11471] Tier 3:
[11472] Analytics
[11473] Historical reporting
[11474] Exact targets should be established during architecture and business-continuity planning.
[11476] 9.64 DATA REPLICATION
[11477] Replication may support:
[11478] Read scaling
[11479] Disaster recovery
[11480] Analytics
[11481] Regional resilience
[11482] However, replicas may lag.
[11483] Critical decisions should use the authoritative source when stale data could create financial, clinical, or security risk.
[11485] 9.65 DATA MIGRATION
[11486] Legacy migration should follow:
[11487] DISCOVER
[11488] ↓
[11489] MAP
[11490] ↓
[11491] PROFILE
[11492] ↓
[11493] CLEAN
[11494] ↓
[11495] TRANSFORM
[11496] ↓
[11497] VALIDATE
[11498] ↓
[11499] RECONCILE
[11500] ↓
[11501] MIGRATE
[11502] ↓
[11503] VERIFY
[11504] ↓
[11505] SIGN-OFF
[11506] Migration must preserve historical relationships.
[11508] 9.66 MIGRATION RECONCILIATION
[11509] After migration:
[11510] Legacy Members
[11511] =
[11512] New Members
[11514] Legacy Claims
[11515] =
[11516] New Claims
[11518] Legacy Financial Balances
[11519] =
[11520] New Financial Balances
[11521] Differences must be investigated and documented.
[11523] 9.67 DATA ARCHIVAL
[11524] Old data may move to lower-cost storage while remaining retrievable.
[11525] Architecture:
[11526] HOT DATA
[11527] ↓
[11528] WARM DATA
[11529] ↓
[11530] COLD/ARCHIVE DATA
[11531] Archived data must remain protected and traceable.
[11533] 9.68 DATA OBSERVABILITY
[11534] Monitor:
[11535] Database latency
[11536] Query performance
[11537] Replication lag
[11538] Storage capacity
[11539] Connection pool usage
[11540] Failed transactions
[11541] Data-quality errors
[11542] Duplicate rates
[11543] Import failures
[11544] Export activity
[11546] 9.69 DATA SECURITY MONITORING
[11547] Monitor:
[11548] Unusual data access
[11549] Mass downloads
[11550] Large queries
[11551] Sensitive-field access
[11552] Cross-tenant attempts
[11553] Unusual exports
[11554] Repeated failed queries
[11555] Privilege changes
[11556] Suspicious service-account activity
[11558] 9.70 DATA EDGE-CASE MATRIX
[11560] 9.71 UNKNOWN DATA EDGE-CASE STRATEGY
[11561] For an unexpected data condition:
[11562] UNKNOWN DATA STATE
[11563] ↓
[11564] DO NOT GUESS
[11565] ↓
[11566] DO NOT OVERWRITE
[11567] ↓
[11568] PRESERVE EXISTING DATA
[11569] ↓
[11570] QUARANTINE IF NECESSARY
[11571] ↓
[11572] LOG
[11573] ↓
[11574] AUDIT
[11575] ↓
[11576] ALERT
[11577] ↓
[11578] CONTROLLED RECOVERY
[11579] Data integrity takes priority over convenience.
[11581] 9.72 DATA ACCESS LAYERS
[11582] Data access should pass through controlled layers:
[11583] User
[11584] ↓
[11585] API
[11586] ↓
[11587] Authorization
[11588] ↓
[11589] Application Service
[11590] ↓
[11591] Data Access Policy
[11592] ↓
[11593] Repository
[11594] ↓
[11595] Database
[11596] Direct unrestricted database access should not be provided to ordinary application users.
[11598] 9.73 DATA GOVERNANCE BOARD
[11599] The organization should establish governance involving appropriate representatives from:
[11600] Executive management
[11601] IT
[11602] Security
[11603] Compliance
[11604] Medical
[11605] Claims
[11606] Finance
[11607] Membership
[11608] Provider management
[11609] Customer service
[11610] Data/analytics
[11611] Responsibilities:
[11612] Data standards

[11614] Ownership
[11615] Retention
[11616] Quality
[11617] Critical data definitions
[11618] Change governance
[11620] 9.74 DATA DICTIONARY
[11621] The Master Blueprint should eventually produce a formal enterprise data dictionary.
[11622] For every important field:
[11623] Field Name
[11624] Business Name
[11625] Definition
[11626] Data Type
[11627] Required?
[11628] Classification
[11629] Owner
[11630] Allowed Values
[11631] Validation Rules
[11632] Source
[11633] Consumers
[11634] Retention
[11635] Sensitivity
[11636] Example:
[11637] member_id
[11639] Definition:
[11640] Unique immutable identifier for an HMO member.
[11642] Type:
[11643] UUID
[11645] Required:
[11646] Yes
[11648] Classification:
[11649] Restricted
[11651] Owner:
[11652] Membership
[11654] Mutable:
[11655] No
[11657] 9.75 DATA LINEAGE
[11658] Critical information should be traceable from origin to usage.
[11659] Example:
[11660] Employer Upload
[11661] ↓
[11662] Employee Record
[11663] ↓
[11664] Member Enrollment
[11665] ↓
[11666] Eligibility
[11667] ↓
[11668] Authorization
[11669] ↓
[11670] Claim
[11671] ↓
[11672] Adjudication
[11673] ↓
[11674] Payment
[11675] ↓
[11676] Financial Report
[11677] This allows the organization to answer:
[11678] Where did this number come from?
[11679] and:
[11680] Which transactions were affected when this source record changed?
[11682] 9.76 DATA QUALITY DASHBOARD
[11683] Management should be able to monitor:
[11684] Duplicate members
[11685] Duplicate providers
[11686] Missing information
[11687] Invalid records
[11688] Expired credentials
[11689] Invalid plans
[11690] Broken relationships
[11691] Failed imports
[11692] Reconciliation differences
[11693] Data-quality trends
[11695] 9.77 MASTER DATA CHANGE WORKFLOW
[11696] Sensitive master-data changes should follow:
[11697] CHANGE REQUEST
[11698] ↓
[11699] VALIDATION
[11700] ↓
[11701] IMPACT ANALYSIS
[11702] ↓
[11703] APPROVAL IF REQUIRED
[11704] ↓
[11705] CHANGE
[11706] ↓
[11707] AUDIT
[11708] ↓
[11709] DOWNSTREAM UPDATE
[11710] ↓
[11711] VERIFICATION
[11713] 9.78 CRITICAL DATA CHANGE EXAMPLE
[11714] Changing a provider bank account:
[11715] Request
[11716] ↓
[11717] Identity verification
[11718] ↓
[11719] Document verification
[11720] ↓
[11721] Bank information validation
[11722] ↓
[11723] Risk check
[11724] ↓
[11725] Approval
[11726] ↓
[11727] Effective date
[11728] ↓
[11729] Audit
[11730] ↓
[11731] Notify relevant systems
[11732] The person changing the account should not automatically be allowed to approve the change.
[11734] 9.79 PART IX SECURITY REQUIREMENTS
[11735] The data architecture must provide:
[11736] Data classification.
[11737] Encryption.
[11738] Access control.
[11739] Field-level protection.
[11740] Tenant isolation.
[11741] Auditability.
[11742] Secure backups.

[11748] Legal holds.
[11749] Data-quality controls.
[11750] Privileged-access controls.
[11752] 9.80 PART IX PERFORMANCE REQUIREMENTS
[11753] The data layer should support:
[11754] Indexed queries
[11755] Pagination
[11756] Query optimization
[11757] Connection pooling
[11758] Read replicas where appropriate
[11759] Caching
[11760] Asynchronous processing
[11761] Partitioning when justified
[11762] Batch operations
[11763] Efficient archival
[11764] No application should perform uncontrolled full-table scans against large operational datasets.
[11766] 9.81 PART IX SCALABILITY REQUIREMENTS
[11767] The architecture should scale across:
[11768] Members
[11769] Providers
[11770] Employers
[11771] Claims
[11772] Claim lines
[11773] Transactions
[11774] Documents
[11775] Audit records
[11776] Events
[11777] Users
[11778] Notifications
[11779] Growth should be accommodated primarily through horizontal scaling and appropriate data-partitioning strategies.
[11781] 9.82 PART IX RELIABILITY REQUIREMENTS
[11782] The data platform must support:
[11783] Backups
[11784] Replication
[11785] Recovery
[11786] Transaction integrity
[11787] Idempotency
[11788] Concurrency control
[11789] Monitoring
[11790] Alerting
[11791] Reconciliation
[11792] Disaster recovery
[11794] 9.83 PART IX USABILITY REQUIREMENTS
[11795] Data should be presented consistently across modules.
[11796] For example, a user should not see:
[11797] Provider Name:
[11798] ABC Hospital
[11799] in one module and:
[11800] ABC HOSPITAL LTD.
[11801] in another simply because different systems maintain independent copies.
[11802] Master data should drive consistent presentation.
[11804] 9.84 PART IX COMPLETION CRITERIA
[11805] Part IX is complete when the platform has:
[11806] Defined enterprise data domains.
[11807] Defined master entities.
[11808] Defined ownership.
[11809] Defined data classification.
[11810] Defined historical/versioning rules.
[11811] Defined data-quality rules.
[11812] Defined tenant isolation.
[11813] Defined retention.
[11814] Defined deletion.

[11816] Defined backup.

[11818] Defined audit data.
[11819] Defined security-event data.
[11820] Defined data lineage.
[11821] Defined data dictionary requirements.
[11822] Defined concurrency strategy.
[11823] Defined consistency requirements.
[11824] Defined unknown-edge-case handling.
[11826] 9.85 PART IX SUMMARY
[11827] The data architecture becomes the foundation upon which the rest of the HMO platform operates.
[11828] The central relationship is:
[11829] IDENTITY
[11830] ↓
[11831] ORGANIZATION
[11832] ↓
[11833] EMPLOYER / PROVIDER
[11834] ↓
[11835] MEMBER
[11836] ↓
[11837] ENROLLMENT
[11838] ↓
[11839] PLAN VERSION
[11840] ↓
[11841] BENEFIT
[11842] ↓
[11843] ELIGIBILITY
[11844] ↓
[11845] AUTHORIZATION
[11846] ↓
[11847] CLAIM
[11848] ↓
[11849] ADJUDICATION
[11850] ↓
[11851] FINANCE
[11852] ↓
[11853] PAYMENT
[11854] ↓
[11855] RECONCILIATION
[11856] ↓
[11857] AUDIT
[11858] The most important principle is:
[11859] Historical data must remain understandable even after the business rules, contracts, plans, users, providers, and organizational structures that created it have changed.
[11860] This allows the HMO to answer not only:
[11861] “What is the current state?”
[11862] but also:
[11863] “What was the state at the exact time this healthcare, financial, or operational decision occurred, and why?”
[11864] That capability is fundamental to an enterprise HMO platform.
[11865] COMPLETE HMO MASTER BLUEPRINT
[11866] PART X — ENTERPRISE TECHNICAL ARCHITECTURE & INFRASTRUCTURE
[11868] 10.0 Purpose
[11869] Part X defines the technical architecture and infrastructure foundation required to operate the enterprise HMO platform reliably at scale.
[11870] The architecture must support:
[11871] Large numbers of members
[11872] Multiple employers
[11873] Multiple healthcare providers
[11874] Internal HMO departments
[11875] Thousands of employees and users
[11876] High-volume claims
[11877] Real-time eligibility checks
[11878] Preauthorization workflows
[11879] Financial transactions
[11880] Sensitive healthcare information
[11881] External integrations
[11882] Background processing
[11883] Reporting and analytics
[11884] Strict role and privilege enforcement
[11885] Complete auditability
[11886] The architecture must implement the five invisible pillars:
[11887] Performance + Security + Scalability + Reliability + Usability
[11888] And throughout every technical layer:
[11889] No request should receive access merely because the user is authenticated.
[11890] Authentication identifies the caller.
[11891] Authorization determines what the caller may do.
[11892] Data scope determines what the caller may see or modify.
[11894] 10.1 Architecture Goals
[11895] The technical architecture shall provide:
[11896] High availability
[11897] Horizontal scalability
[11898] Strong tenant isolation
[11899] Centralized identity and authorization
[11900] Secure API access
[11901] Reliable asynchronous processing
[11902] Transaction integrity
[11903] Complete observability
[11904] Disaster recovery
[11905] Controlled deployments
[11906] Safe integrations
[11907] Strong data protection
[11908] Low-latency critical operations
[11909] Resilient background processing
[11910] Explainable business decisions
[11911] Controlled administrative access
[11912] Auditability of sensitive actions
[11913] Safe failure and recovery
[11915] 10.2 Architecture Principles
[11916] Principle 1 — Security by Design
[11917] Security must not be added after development.
[11918] Every component must be designed with:
[11919] authentication
[11920] authorization
[11921] encryption
[11922] auditability
[11923] input validation
[11924] secure configuration
[11925] least privilege
[11926] threat protection
[11928] Principle 2 — Deny by Default
[11929] If the system cannot confidently determine that an action is authorized:
[11930] DENY
[11931] Examples:
[11932] Unknown user → deny
[11933] Invalid token → deny
[11934] Expired session → deny
[11935] Missing permission → deny
[11936] Missing data scope → deny
[11937] Suspended account → deny
[11938] Unknown tenant → deny
[11939] Authorization service unavailable for a sensitive action → deny or place into a safe review state
[11941] 10.3 Logical Architecture
[11942] The platform shall use a layered architecture.
[11943] USERS / SYSTEMS
[11944] │
[11945] ▼
[11946] ┌─────────────────────┐
[11947] │ CDN / WAF / Firewall │
[11948] └──────────┬──────────┘
[11949] │
[11950] ▼
[11951] ┌───────────────┐
[11952] │ API Gateway   │
[11953] └───────┬───────┘
[11954] │
[11955] ┌───────────▼───────────┐
[11956] │ Identity & Access     │
[11957] │ Authentication        │
[11958] │ RBAC / ABAC / SoD     │
[11959] └───────────┬───────────┘
[11960] │
[11961] ▼
[11962] ┌────────────────────┐
[11963] │ Application Layer  │
[11964] └─────────┬──────────┘
[11965] │
[11966] ┌─────────────────┼──────────────────┐
[11967] │                 │                  │
[11968] ▼                 ▼                  ▼
[11969] HMO DOMAIN       FINANCIAL DOMAIN   EXPERIENCE DOMAIN

[11988] │

[11992] 10.4 Major Architectural Layers

[11994] Layer 1 — Presentation
[11995] Web application

[11998] Provider portal

[12000] Mobile application where required

[12028] Notifications

[12030] Relational database

[12041] Alerts

[12047] Certificates
[12048] Monitoring
[12049] Backup
[12050] Disaster recovery
[12052] 10.5 Architecture Style Decision
[12053] The platform should avoid automatically adopting dozens of microservices simply because it is an enterprise application.
[12054] A recommended approach is:
[12055] Domain-oriented modular architecture with service extraction capability.
[12056] Initially, tightly related functionality may be deployed together while maintaining strict internal module boundaries.
[12057] As scale and operational requirements justify it, modules can be extracted into independent services.
[12058] This provides:
[12059] lower initial complexity
[12060] easier development
[12061] simpler debugging
[12062] fewer distributed transactions
[12063] easier local development
[12064] lower infrastructure overhead
[12065] while preserving the ability to scale high-volume domains independently.
[12067] 10.6 Domain Boundaries
[12068] The platform should establish clear domain ownership.
[12069] Identity & Access
[12070] Responsible for:
[12071] users
[12072] authentication
[12073] MFA
[12074] roles
[12075] permissions
[12076] policies
[12077] sessions
[12078] devices
[12079] authorization decisions
[12080] Organization
[12081] Responsible for:
[12082] HMO organization
[12083] regions
[12084] branches
[12085] departments
[12086] teams
[12087] organizational hierarchy
[12088] Employer
[12089] Responsible for:
[12090] corporate clients
[12091] employees
[12092] contracts
[12093] employer users
[12094] enrollment files
[12095] Member
[12096] Responsible for:
[12097] members
[12098] dependants
[12099] profiles
[12100] identifiers
[12101] membership lifecycle
[12102] Provider
[12103] Responsible for:
[12104] providers
[12105] locations
[12106] credentials
[12107] contracts
[12108] networks
[12109] provider users
[12110] Plan & Benefits
[12111] Responsible for:
[12112] plans
[12113] versions
[12114] benefits
[12115] exclusions
[12116] limits
[12117] tariffs
[12118] copays
[12119] rules
[12120] Enrollment & Eligibility
[12121] Responsible for:
[12122] enrollment
[12123] effective dates
[12124] eligibility
[12125] coverage
[12126] Healthcare
[12127] Responsible for:
[12128] service requests
[12129] referrals
[12130] preauthorization
[12131] medical review
[12132] utilization
[12133] Claims
[12134] Responsible for:
[12135] claims
[12136] claim lines
[12137] adjudication
[12138] claim review
[12139] disputes
[12140] Finance
[12141] Responsible for:
[12142] billing
[12143] payments
[12144] settlements
[12145] reconciliation
[12146] refunds
[12147] ledger
[12148] Customer Experience
[12149] Responsible for:
[12150] cases
[12151] complaints
[12152] support
[12153] conversations
[12154] knowledge base
[12155] Notification
[12156] Responsible for:
[12157] SMS
[12158] email
[12159] push
[12160] in-app notifications
[12161] Document
[12162] Responsible for:
[12163] document storage
[12164] document metadata
[12165] document scanning
[12166] document lifecycle
[12167] Audit & Security
[12168] Responsible for:
[12169] audit trail
[12170] security events
[12171] access monitoring
[12172] administrative activity
[12173] Analytics
[12174] Responsible for:
[12175] reporting
[12176] dashboards
[12177] analytics
[12178] data warehouse
[12179] business intelligence
[12180] Integration
[12181] Responsible for:
[12182] external APIs
[12183] webhooks
[12184] provider integrations
[12185] payment integrations
[12186] communication providers
[12188] 10.7 API Gateway
[12189] All external APIs should pass through a controlled API gateway.
[12190] Responsibilities include:
[12191] authentication enforcement
[12192] token validation
[12193] request routing
[12194] rate limiting
[12195] throttling
[12196] request size limits
[12197] API versioning
[12198] correlation IDs
[12199] threat detection
[12200] request logging
[12201] response policies
[12202] IP restrictions where required
[12203] The gateway must not be the only authorization layer.
[12204] Business services must independently enforce authorization.
[12206] 10.8 API Security Architecture
[12207] APIs should follow consistent standards.
[12208] Authentication
[12209] Recommended mechanisms include:
[12210] OAuth 2.0
[12211] OpenID Connect
[12212] PKCE for public clients
[12213] short-lived access tokens
[12214] refresh-token rotation where applicable
[12215] service-to-service credentials
[12216] certificate-based authentication where appropriate
[12217] Authorization
[12218] Every protected endpoint must determine:
[12219] WHO?
[12220] WHAT?
[12221] WHICH RESOURCE?
[12222] WHICH ACTION?
[12223] WHICH ORGANIZATION?
[12224] WHICH DATA SCOPE?
[12225] UNDER WHICH BUSINESS RULE?
[12227] 10.9 API Naming
[12228] Example:
[12229] GET    /api/v1/members/{memberId}
[12230] POST   /api/v1/members
[12231] PATCH  /api/v1/members/{memberId}
[12233] GET    /api/v1/claims/{claimId}
[12234] POST   /api/v1/claims
[12235] POST   /api/v1/claims/{claimId}/submit
[12237] GET    /api/v1/providers/{providerId}
[12238] POST   /api/v1/preauthorizations
[12239] POST   /api/v1/payments
[12240] Sensitive operations should use explicit action endpoints rather than ambiguous generic updates.
[12241] For example:
[12242] POST /claims/{id}/approve
[12243] is preferable to:
[12244] PATCH /claims/{id}
[12245] {
[12246] "status": "approved"
[12247] }
[12248] because the former allows dedicated:
[12249] permission checks
[12250] SoD checks
[12251] approval validation
[12252] audit events
[12253] workflow validation
[12254] business rules
[12256] 10.10 API Versioning
[12257] APIs should support controlled versioning.
[12258] Example:
[12259] /api/v1/
[12260] /api/v2/
[12261] Breaking changes must not silently alter existing consumers.
[12262] API changes should go through:
[12263] Design
[12264] Compatibility assessment
[12265] Security review
[12266] Documentation
[12267] Testing
[12268] Versioning
[12269] Deprecation period
[12270] Migration
[12271] Retirement
[12273] 10.11 API Request Standards
[12274] Every request should support, where applicable:
[12275] correlation ID
[12276] request ID
[12277] idempotency key
[12278] authentication token
[12279] pagination
[12280] filtering
[12281] sorting
[12282] timeout policy
[12283] validation
[12284] Example:
[12285] X-Correlation-ID
[12286] X-Request-ID
[12287] Idempotency-Key
[12288] Authorization
[12290] 10.12 API Error Standard
[12291] Errors should have a predictable structure.
[12292] Example:
[12293] {
[12294] "error": {
[12295] "code": "CLAIM_ALREADY_APPROVED",
[12296] "message": "The claim cannot be modified after approval.",
[12297] "correlationId": "abc-123",
[12298] "retryable": false
[12299] }
[12300] }
[12301] Never expose:
[12302] database errors
[12303] stack traces
[12304] internal hostnames
[12305] secrets
[12306] internal architecture details
[12307] sensitive medical information
[12309] 10.13 Pagination
[12310] Large collections must never be returned without controlled pagination.
[12311] Example:
[12312] ?page=1&pageSize=50
[12313] or preferably cursor-based pagination for very large datasets.
[12314] Maximum page size must be enforced server-side.
[12316] 10.14 Idempotency
[12317] Critical operations must support idempotency.
[12318] Examples:
[12319] payment
[12320] claim submission
[12321] enrollment
[12322] refund
[12323] settlement
[12324] authorization request
[12325] webhook processing
[12326] Example:
[12327] Idempotency-Key: 9c7e...
[12328] If the same request is received multiple times:
[12329] The system must not create multiple financial or business transactions.
[12331] 10.15 Event & Messaging Architecture
[12332] The platform should use asynchronous messaging for long-running or non-critical synchronous processes.
[12333] Examples:
[12334] ClaimSubmitted
[12335] ClaimValidated
[12336] ClaimApproved
[12337] PaymentInitiated
[12338] PaymentCompleted
[12339] MemberEnrolled
[12340] ProviderCredentialExpiring
[12341] AuthorizationApproved
[12342] ComplaintEscalated
[12343] NotificationRequested
[12345] 10.16 Event Architecture
[12346] Recommended flow:
[12347] Business Transaction
[12348] │
[12349] ▼
[12350] Database Transaction
[12351] │
[12352] ▼
[12353] Outbox Event
[12354] │
[12355] ▼
[12356] Message Broker
[12357] │
[12358] ├──► Notification
[12359] ├──► Analytics
[12360] ├──► Audit
[12361] ├──► Reporting
[12362] └──► Other Domain Services
[12363] The transactional outbox pattern should be used where consistency between database state and event publication is important.
[12365] 10.17 Message Broker
[12366] A suitable enterprise message infrastructure may use technologies such as:
[12367] Kafka
[12368] managed queue services
[12369] pub/sub systems
[12370] The exact technology should be determined through architecture review.
[12371] The broker must support:
[12372] durable messages
[12373] retries
[12374] dead-letter queues
[12375] consumer groups
[12376] monitoring
[12377] message ordering where required
[12378] replay where appropriate
[12379] schema versioning
[12381] 10.18 Event Naming
[12382] Events should use business terminology.
[12383] Good:
[12384] MemberEnrolled
[12385] ClaimSubmitted
[12386] ClaimApproved
[12387] PaymentReconciled
[12388] ProviderSuspended
[12389] Avoid technical-only events such as:
[12390] DatabaseRowUpdated
[12391] RecordChanged
[12392] TableModified
[12393] Business events should communicate meaningful domain changes.
[12395] 10.19 Event Delivery
[12396] Events may be:
[12397] duplicated
[12398] delayed
[12399] reordered
[12400] lost if infrastructure is misconfigured
[12401] delivered after the underlying business state has changed
[12402] Therefore consumers must be:
[12403] Idempotent and state-aware.
[12405] 10.20 Dead-Letter Queue
[12406] Messages that repeatedly fail must move to a dead-letter queue.
[12407] Example:
[12408] Message
[12409] ↓
[12410] Attempt 1
[12411] ↓
[12412] Failure
[12413] ↓
[12414] Retry
[12415] ↓
[12416] Attempt 2
[12417] ↓
[12418] Failure
[12419] ↓
[12420] Retry
[12421] ↓
[12422] Attempt 3
[12423] ↓
[12424] Failure
[12425] ↓
[12426] DLQ
[12427] DLQ processing must include:
[12428] alerting
[12429] diagnostic information
[12430] failure reason
[12431] correlation ID
[12432] original event
[12433] retry count
[12434] timestamp
[12435] controlled replay capability
[12437] 10.21 Database Architecture
[12438] The primary transactional database should use a relational database suitable for strong consistency.
[12439] A PostgreSQL-based architecture is a strong reference option.
[12440] Core transactional domains include:
[12441] members
[12442] employers
[12443] providers
[12444] plans
[12445] enrollment
[12446] eligibility
[12447] authorization
[12448] claims
[12449] finance
[12450] cases
[12451] identity
[12452] permissions
[12453] audit metadata
[12455] 10.22 Database Principles
[12456] The database architecture must support:
[12457] ACID transactions
[12458] foreign keys
[12459] unique constraints
[12460] check constraints
[12461] indexes
[12462] partitioning where appropriate
[12463] optimistic locking
[12464] transaction isolation
[12465] encryption
[12466] backup
[12467] replication
[12468] point-in-time recovery
[12470] 10.23 Database Ownership
[12471] Each domain must have clear ownership of its data.
[12472] A service should not directly manipulate another domain's tables merely because it has database access.
[12473] Instead:
[12474] Service A
[12475] ↓
[12476] Service/API/Event
[12477] ↓
[12478] Service B
[12479] ↓
[12480] Service B Database
[12481] This protects domain boundaries.
[12483] 10.24 Database Connection Management
[12484] Production applications must use controlled connection pooling.
[12485] Example architecture:
[12486] Application Instances
[12487] ↓
[12488] Connection Pool
[12489] ↓
[12490] Database
[12491] Without pooling, sudden traffic increases can exhaust database connections.
[12493] 10.25 Read Replicas
[12494] Read-heavy operations may use read replicas.
[12495] Examples:
[12496] provider directory
[12497] member search
[12498] reporting
[12499] dashboards
[12500] historical claim searches
[12501] However:
[12502] Critical authorization, financial, and transactional decisions must not depend on stale data where stale data could produce an unsafe outcome.
[12504] 10.26 Redis / Distributed Cache
[12505] Redis or an equivalent distributed cache can support:
[12506] session data
[12507] short-lived eligibility results
[12508] provider directory caching
[12509] configuration caching
[12510] rate limiting
[12511] authorization policy caching
[12512] frequently accessed reference data
[12513] Cache must never become the authoritative source for critical financial or clinical history.
[12515] 10.27 Cache Invalidation
[12516] Cache invalidation must be explicitly designed.
[12517] Example:
[12518] Plan Updated
[12519] ↓
[12520] Database Updated
[12521] ↓
[12522] PlanChanged Event
[12523] ↓
[12524] Cache Invalidation
[12525] ↓
[12526] New Version Loaded
[12527] Sensitive authorization caches require especially careful invalidation.
[12529] 10.28 Search Architecture
[12530] A dedicated search engine may be used for:
[12531] member search
[12532] provider search
[12533] claims search
[12534] case search
[12535] document metadata search
[12536] operational dashboards
[12537] Search indexes must preserve authorization boundaries.
[12538] A user must never retrieve a record through search that they could not retrieve through the main application.
[12540] 10.29 Object & Document Storage
[12541] Documents should be stored separately from transactional records.
[12542] Possible architecture:
[12543] Application
[12544] ↓
[12545] Document Service
[12546] ↓
[12547] Malware Scan
[12548] ↓
[12549] Object Storage
[12550] ↓
[12551] Encrypted File
[12552] Documents may include:
[12553] identification documents
[12554] provider licenses
[12555] contracts
[12556] medical documents
[12557] claim attachments
[12558] invoices
[12559] correspondence
[12561] 10.30 Document Security
[12562] Every document must have:
[12563] document ID
[12564] owner
[12565] organization
[12566] classification
[12567] document type
[12568] access policy
[12569] creation timestamp
[12570] version
[12571] retention policy
[12572] checksum
[12573] audit history
[12574] Direct public object-storage URLs should not be used for sensitive documents.
[12575] Access should occur through an authorization-controlled service.
[12577] 10.31 Cloud Infrastructure
[12578] The architecture should remain cloud-agnostic at the design level while allowing a reference implementation on a major cloud platform.
[12579] An AWS-style reference architecture could include equivalents such as:
[12580] CloudFront/CDN
[12581] ↓
[12582] WAF
[12583] ↓
[12584] Load Balancer / API Gateway
[12585] ↓
[12586] Container / Kubernetes / Managed Compute
[12587] ↓
[12588] PostgreSQL
[12589] Redis
[12590] Message Broker
[12591] Object Storage
[12592] Search
[12593] Analytics
[12594] Monitoring
[12595] Secrets
[12596] KMS
[12597] The exact infrastructure products should be selected based on:
[12598] expected scale
[12599] team expertise
[12600] cost
[12601] availability requirements
[12602] operational complexity
[12603] regulatory requirements
[12604] data-location requirements
[12605] integration needs
[12607] 10.32 Network Architecture
[12608] The production environment should use network segmentation.
[12609] Example:
[12610] INTERNET
[12611] │
[12612] ▼
[12613] WAF / CDN
[12614] │
[12615] ▼
[12616] Public Edge
[12617] │
[12618] ┌──────▼──────┐
[12619] │ Application │
[12620] │   Network  │
[12621] └──────┬──────┘
[12622] │
[12623] ┌─────────┼──────────┐
[12624] ▼         ▼          ▼
[12625] Database   Cache      Broker
[12626] Private   Private     Private
[12627] Databases should not be directly accessible from the public internet.
[12629] 10.33 Environment Architecture
[12630] Recommended environments:
[12631] LOCAL
[12632] ↓
[12633] DEVELOPMENT
[12634] ↓
[12635] TEST
[12636] ↓
[12637] STAGING
[12638] ↓
[12639] UAT
[12640] ↓
[12641] PRODUCTION
[12642] Additional environments may include:
[12643] integration
[12644] sandbox
[12645] disaster recovery
[12646] Production credentials must never be reused in lower environments.
[12648] 10.34 Infrastructure as Code
[12649] Infrastructure should be defined using Infrastructure as Code.
[12650] Terraform or an equivalent tool can manage:
[12651] networks
[12652] compute
[12653] databases
[12654] queues
[12655] storage
[12656] monitoring
[12657] security policies
[12658] secrets infrastructure
[12659] DNS
[12660] load balancing
[12661] Infrastructure changes should be version-controlled.
[12663] 10.35 CI/CD Architecture
[12664] Recommended deployment pipeline:
[12665] Developer
[12666] ↓
[12667] Git Repository
[12668] ↓
[12669] Pull Request
[12670] ↓
[12671] Code Review
[12672] ↓
[12673] Unit Tests
[12674] ↓
[12675] SAST
[12676] ↓
[12677] Dependency Scan
[12678] ↓
[12679] Build
[12680] ↓
[12681] Container Scan
[12682] ↓
[12683] Integration Tests
[12684] ↓
[12685] Deploy to Test
[12686] ↓
[12687] Acceptance Tests
[12688] ↓
[12689] Security Tests
[12690] ↓
[12691] Approval
[12692] ↓
[12693] Production Deployment
[12695] 10.36 Deployment Strategies
[12696] Depending on service criticality, use:
[12697] rolling deployment
[12698] blue/green deployment
[12699] canary deployment
[12700] High-risk services such as:
[12701] finance
[12702] claims adjudication
[12703] identity
[12704] authorization
[12705] should have stricter deployment controls.
[12707] 10.37 Database Migration Safety
[12708] Database migrations must be backward compatible where possible.
[12709] Recommended approach:
[12710] Expand
[12711] ↓
[12712] Deploy Compatible Application
[12713] ↓
[12714] Migrate Data
[12715] ↓
[12716] Switch Application Behaviour
[12717] ↓
[12718] Contract
[12719] Avoid deployments that simultaneously require:
[12720] destructive schema changes
[12721] application downtime
[12722] irreversible data transformations
[12723] unless explicitly approved.
[12725] 10.38 Observability Architecture
[12726] The platform must implement three primary observability layers:
[12727] Metrics
[12728] Examples:
[12729] API latency
[12730] request rate
[12731] error rate
[12732] database latency
[12733] queue depth
[12734] cache hit ratio
[12735] CPU
[12736] memory
[12737] storage
[12738] claim-processing throughput
[12739] Logs
[12740] Examples:
[12741] authentication events
[12742] authorization failures
[12743] application errors
[12744] integration failures
[12745] workflow transitions
[12746] Distributed Traces
[12747] Useful for:
[12748] Member Request
[12749] ↓
[12750] API Gateway
[12751] ↓
[12752] Authorization
[12753] ↓
[12754] Eligibility
[12755] ↓
[12756] Provider Service
[12757] ↓
[12758] Database
[12759] A single correlation ID should follow the request.
[12761] 10.39 Audit vs Application Logs
[12762] These must remain separate concepts.
[12763] Application logs
[12764] Used for:
[12765] debugging
[12766] operations
[12767] performance
[12768] Audit events
[12769] Used for:
[12770] accountability
[12771] compliance
[12772] sensitive business history
[12773] Security events
[12774] Used for:
[12775] intrusion detection
[12776] suspicious access
[12777] privilege escalation
[12778] account compromise
[12779] A user deleting a claim note, for example, may require an audit event even if the operation itself succeeds.
[12781] 10.40 Performance Architecture
[12782] Performance targets should be defined by operation criticality.
[12783] Example target categories:
[12784] These are engineering targets and should be validated through load testing rather than treated as guaranteed production values.
[12786] 10.41 Performance Techniques
[12787] Use:
[12788] caching
[12789] database indexing
[12790] connection pooling
[12791] pagination
[12792] asynchronous processing
[12793] queue-based workloads
[12794] horizontal scaling
[12795] CDN
[12796] query optimization
[12797] precomputed aggregates
[12798] read replicas
[12799] efficient serialization
[12800] Avoid:
[12801] N+1 queries
[12802] unbounded queries
[12803] synchronous processing of large datasets
[12804] unnecessary service-to-service calls
[12805] oversized API responses
[12807] 10.42 Scalability Architecture
[12808] The platform should scale horizontally.
[12809] Example:
[12810] Load Balancer
[12811] │
[12812] ┌────────────┼────────────┐
[12813] ▼            ▼            ▼
[12814] App Node 1   App Node 2   App Node 3
[12815] │            │            │
[12816] └────────────┼────────────┘
[12817] ▼
[12818] Shared Data
[12819] Application instances should remain as stateless as practical.
[12821] 10.43 Autoscaling
[12822] Autoscaling may use:
[12823] CPU
[12824] memory
[12825] request rate
[12826] queue depth
[12827] processing latency
[12828] custom business metrics
[12829] Example:
[12830] If claim-processing queue depth increases significantly:
[12831] Queue Depth ↑
[12832] ↓
[12833] Worker Instances ↑
[12834] ↓
[12835] Processing Capacity ↑
[12836] Autoscaling must have upper limits to prevent uncontrolled infrastructure costs.
[12838] 10.44 Reliability Architecture
[12839] The platform must assume that components will fail.
[12840] Failures may occur in:
[12841] databases
[12842] networks
[12843] APIs
[12844] payment providers
[12845] SMS providers
[12846] email services
[12847] message brokers
[12848] authentication providers
[12849] storage
[12850] DNS
[12851] certificates
[12852] compute nodes
[12853] Therefore every integration should have:
[12854] timeout
[12855] retry policy
[12856] circuit breaker
[12857] idempotency
[12858] monitoring
[12859] fallback where safe
[12861] 10.45 Retry Policy
[12862] Retries must not be unlimited.
[12863] Example:
[12864] Attempt 1
[12865] ↓
[12866] Failure
[12867] ↓
[12868] Wait
[12869] ↓
[12870] Attempt 2
[12871] ↓
[12872] Failure
[12873] ↓
[12874] Wait
[12875] ↓
[12876] Attempt 3
[12877] ↓
[12878] Failure
[12879] ↓
[12880] DLQ / Manual Review
[12881] Retries should use exponential backoff with jitter where appropriate.
[12883] 10.46 Circuit Breaker
[12884] If an external dependency repeatedly fails:
[12885] Normal
[12886] ↓
[12887] Failure Threshold
[12888] ↓
[12889] OPEN
[12890] ↓
[12891] Stop Requests
[12892] ↓
[12893] Recovery Test
[12894] ↓
[12895] HALF OPEN
[12896] ↓
[12897] Success
[12898] ↓
[12899] CLOSED
[12900] This prevents cascading failures.
[12902] 10.47 Graceful Degradation
[12903] Not every failure should bring down the entire platform.
[12904] For example:
[12905] If SMS is unavailable:
[12906] member can still access the portal
[12907] notification may remain queued
[12908] system records delivery failure
[12909] retry occurs later
[12910] But if authorization cannot be verified for a highly sensitive operation:
[12911] The operation must not simply proceed.
[12913] 10.48 Bulkhead Isolation
[12914] High-volume operations should not consume all platform resources.
[12915] For example:
[12916] Claims Workers
[12917] Finance Workers
[12918] Notification Workers
[12919] Report Workers
[12920] should have controlled resource boundaries.
[12921] A large reporting job should not prevent members from checking eligibility.
[12923] 10.49 Disaster Recovery
[12924] The platform must define:
[12925] Recovery Time Objective (RTO)
[12926] Recovery Point Objective (RPO)
[12927] by system tier.
[12928] Example:
[12929] Exact RTO/RPO values must be established through business impact analysis.
[12931] 10.50 Backup Architecture
[12932] Backups should include:
[12933] database backups
[12934] point-in-time recovery
[12935] configuration backups
[12936] infrastructure definitions
[12937] critical object storage
[12938] audit data according to retention policy
[12939] Backups must be:
[12940] encrypted
[12941] monitored
[12942] access-controlled
[12943] periodically restored in testing
[12944] A backup that has never been restored successfully should not be considered fully trusted.
[12946] 10.51 High Availability
[12947] Critical infrastructure should use redundant components.
[12948] Examples:
[12949] Application Node A
[12950] Application Node B
[12952] Database Primary
[12953] Database Replica
[12955] Worker A
[12956] Worker B
[12958] Availability Zone A
[12959] Availability Zone B
[12960] The exact topology depends on availability requirements and cost.
[12962] 10.52 Active-Active vs Active-Passive
[12963] The platform should choose architecture per workload.
[12964] Active-Active
[12965] Useful where:
[12966] continuous availability is required
[12967] workloads can safely operate across multiple locations
[12968] data consistency architecture supports it
[12969] Active-Passive
[12970] Useful where:
[12971] failover complexity must be reduced
[12972] workloads are highly stateful
[12973] cost needs to be controlled
[12974] Not every component needs active-active architecture.
[12976] 10.53 Integration Architecture
[12977] External integrations may include:
[12978] banks
[12979] payment gateways
[12980] SMS providers
[12981] email providers
[12982] push notification platforms
[12983] healthcare providers
[12984] employer systems
[12985] identity providers
[12986] accounting systems
[12987] government or regulatory systems where applicable
[12988] analytics systems
[12989] Each integration must have an integration contract.
[12991] 10.54 Integration Contract
[12992] Each external integration should document:
[12993] endpoint
[12994] authentication
[12995] request schema
[12996] response schema
[12997] timeout
[12998] retry policy
[12999] rate limits
[13000] idempotency
[13001] webhook behavior
[13002] error handling
[13003] reconciliation
[13004] monitoring
[13005] credential rotation
[13006] owner
[13007] escalation contact
[13009] 10.55 External API Failures
[13010] Example:
[13011] HMO → Payment Gateway
[13012] ↓
[13013] Timeout
[13014] ↓
[13015] Unknown payment status
[13016] The system must not automatically mark the payment as failed.
[13017] Instead:
[13018] PENDING / UNKNOWN
[13019] ↓
[13020] Gateway Status Query
[13021] ↓
[13022] Webhook / Confirmation
[13023] ↓
[13024] Final State
[13025] This prevents duplicate payments.
[13027] 10.56 Webhook Architecture
[13028] Webhook processing should include:
[13029] Signature validation
[13030] Authentication
[13031] Schema validation
[13032] Event identification
[13033] Idempotency check
[13034] State validation
[13035] Transaction processing
[13036] Audit event
[13037] Response
[13038] Webhook events should be safely replayable where appropriate.
[13040] 10.57 Batch Processing
[13041] Large operations should run through controlled workers.
[13042] Examples:
[13043] employer enrollment imports
[13044] claims ingestion
[13045] provider updates
[13046] monthly billing
[13047] settlement generation
[13048] statement generation
[13049] report generation
[13050] notification campaigns
[13051] Batch jobs should provide:
[13052] job ID
[13053] status
[13054] progress
[13055] start time
[13056] end time
[13057] initiated by
[13058] authorization context
[13059] errors
[13060] retry count
[13061] result summary
[13063] 10.58 Long-Running Operations
[13064] The API should not hold a connection open for minutes while processing large workloads.
[13065] Instead:
[13066] POST /bulk-import
[13067] ↓
[13068] 202 Accepted
[13069] ↓
[13070] Job ID
[13071] ↓
[13072] Background Worker
[13073] ↓
[13074] Progress
[13075] ↓
[13076] Completion
[13077] Users can retrieve job status.
[13079] 10.59 Authorization Enforcement Points
[13080] Authorization must exist at multiple levels.
[13081] Level 1 — UI
[13082] Hide actions the user cannot perform.
[13083] Level 2 — API
[13084] Verify permissions.
[13085] Level 3 — Domain Service
[13086] Verify business authorization.
[13087] Level 4 — Data Access
[13088] Enforce tenant and scope restrictions.
[13089] Level 5 — Database
[13090] Use technical controls where appropriate.
[13091] Level 6 — Audit
[13092] Record sensitive actions.
[13093] The UI must never be treated as the security boundary.
[13095] 10.60 Financial Architecture Protection
[13096] Financial operations require additional controls.
[13097] For:
[13098] Payment
[13099] Refund
[13100] Settlement
[13101] Write-off
[13102] Bank Account Change
[13103] the architecture should enforce:
[13104] Authentication
[13105] ↓
[13106] Authorization
[13107] ↓
[13108] Scope
[13109] ↓
[13110] Business Validation
[13111] ↓
[13112] SoD
[13113] ↓
[13114] Approval
[13115] ↓
[13116] Execution
[13117] ↓
[13118] Ledger
[13119] ↓
[13120] Reconciliation
[13121] ↓
[13122] Audit
[13124] 10.61 Clinical Architecture Protection
[13125] Clinical workflows require:
[13126] minimum necessary access
[13127] clinical role restrictions
[13128] medical review permissions
[13129] sensitive document controls
[13130] immutable historical decisions where required
[13131] controlled overrides
[13132] auditability
[13133] A technical administrator should not automatically receive unrestricted clinical access merely because they administer the system.
[13135] 10.62 Search Security
[13136] Search must enforce authorization before returning results.
[13137] Bad architecture:
[13138] Search Everything
[13139] ↓
[13140] Filter in UI
[13141] Correct architecture:
[13142] User
[13143] ↓
[13144] Authorization
[13145] ↓
[13146] Data Scope
[13147] ↓
[13148] Authorized Query
[13149] ↓
[13150] Search Results
[13151] This prevents data leakage through search.
[13153] 10.63 Reporting Security
[13154] Reports are also data access operations.
[13155] A user who cannot see provider financial data should not gain access simply by opening a report.
[13156] Therefore report generation must evaluate:
[13157] user
[13158] role
[13159] permission
[13160] organization
[13161] scope
[13162] report classification
[13163] data classification
[13165] 10.64 Export Security
[13166] Exports require additional controls.
[13167] Examples:
[13168] CSV
[13169] Excel
[13170] PDF
[13171] API exports
[13172] Controls should include:
[13173] export permission
[13174] data scope
[13175] field masking
[13176] record limits
[13177] reason where required
[13178] audit
[13179] rate limiting
[13180] download expiry
[13181] secure file storage
[13183] 10.65 Feature Flags
[13184] Feature flags should control:
[13185] new functionality
[13186] beta features
[13187] migration stages
[13188] emergency disablement
[13189] tenant-specific features
[13190] Feature flags themselves require authorization.
[13191] A normal administrator should not automatically have permission to activate a high-risk financial feature.
[13193] 10.66 Configuration Management
[13194] Configuration should be separated into:
[13195] Static configuration
[13196] Examples:
[13197] service endpoints
[13198] infrastructure configuration
[13199] Business configuration
[13200] Examples:
[13201] approval thresholds
[13202] benefit limits
[13203] notification templates
[13204] SLA values
[13205] Secrets
[13206] Examples:
[13207] passwords
[13208] API keys
[13209] private keys
[13210] certificates
[13211] Secrets must never be stored directly in source code.
[13213] 10.67 Secrets Management
[13214] Secrets should use a dedicated secrets management system.
[13215] Requirements:
[13216] encryption
[13217] access control
[13218] rotation
[13219] expiration
[13220] audit
[13221] versioning
[13222] emergency revocation
[13224] 10.68 Certificate Management
[13225] Certificates should be monitored for expiry.
[13226] Example alert:
[13227] Certificate expires in 30 days
[13228] ↓
[13229] Warning
[13231] Certificate expires in 14 days
[13232] ↓
[13233] High Priority
[13235] Certificate expires in 7 days
[13236] ↓
[13237] Critical
[13239] Certificate expired
[13240] ↓
[13241] Service Failure
[13242] Certificate rotation should be automated where practical.
[13244] 10.69 Time Synchronization
[13245] Distributed systems must maintain synchronized clocks.
[13246] Clock differences can affect:
[13247] tokens
[13248] authorization
[13249] audit records
[13250] payment timestamps
[13251] claim deadlines
[13252] SLA calculations
[13253] event ordering
[13254] Infrastructure should use reliable time synchronization.
[13256] 10.70 Infrastructure Edge-Case Matrix
[13258] 10.71 Unknown Infrastructure Edge-Case Strategy
[13259] The system cannot realistically predict every possible infrastructure failure.
[13260] Therefore the architecture must follow a universal failure strategy:
[13261] Unexpected Condition
[13262] ↓
[13263] Identify Whether Operation Is Safe
[13264] ↓
[13265] If Unsafe → STOP / DENY
[13266] ↓
[13267] Preserve Current State
[13268] ↓
[13269] Generate Correlation ID
[13270] ↓
[13271] Record Diagnostic Information
[13272] ↓
[13273] Generate Security / Operational Event
[13274] ↓
[13275] Alert Appropriate Team
[13276] ↓
[13277] Move to Safe Recovery State
[13278] ↓
[13279] Retry / Repair / Manual Review
[13280] ↓
[13281] Audit Final Outcome
[13282] The platform must never treat:
[13283] “We did not anticipate this error”
[13284] as justification for unsafe behaviour.
[13286] 10.72 Security Testing Architecture
[13287] Security testing should include:
[13288] Application Security
[13289] SAST
[13290] DAST
[13291] dependency scanning
[13292] API security testing
[13293] authentication testing
[13294] authorization testing
[13295] Infrastructure Security
[13296] vulnerability scanning
[13297] container scanning
[13298] configuration scanning
[13299] network security testing
[13300] Access Control Testing
[13301] Explicitly test:
[13302] horizontal privilege escalation
[13303] vertical privilege escalation
[13304] IDOR
[13305] cross-tenant access
[13306] role bypass
[13307] scope bypass
[13308] bulk-export bypass
[13309] API-only privilege escalation
[13311] 10.73 Performance Testing
[13312] Testing should include:
[13313] Load Testing
[13314] Normal expected traffic.
[13315] Stress Testing
[13316] Traffic beyond expected capacity.
[13317] Spike Testing
[13318] Sudden traffic increase.
[13319] Endurance Testing
[13320] Long-running traffic.
[13321] Failover Testing
[13322] Infrastructure failure.
[13323] Recovery Testing
[13324] System recovery after failure.
[13326] 10.74 Capacity Planning
[13327] Capacity planning should monitor:
[13328] members
[13329] providers
[13330] employers
[13331] employees
[13332] claims/day
[13333] claims/month
[13334] API requests/second
[13335] eligibility requests/second
[13336] documents/day
[13337] notifications/day
[13338] concurrent users
[13339] database size
[13340] storage growth
[13341] Capacity assumptions should be reviewed periodically.
[13343] 10.75 Technical SLO Framework
[13344] The platform should define service-level objectives.
[13345] Examples:
[13346] Availability
[13347] Critical services:
[13348] Target ≥ 99.9%, subject to approved business requirements.
[13349] API latency
[13350] Critical APIs:
[13351] Defined latency percentile targets rather than average latency alone.
[13352] Error rate
[13353] Track:
[13354] HTTP 5xx
[13355] failed jobs
[13356] failed integrations
[13357] authorization errors
[13358] database failures
[13359] Recovery
[13360] Monitor:
[13361] recovery time
[13362] failed deployments
[13363] incident frequency
[13364] recovery success
[13366] 10.76 Architecture Governance
[13367] Architecture changes should use Architecture Decision Records.
[13368] Each ADR should document:
[13369] Decision
[13370] Context
[13371] Options
[13372] Chosen Option
[13373] Reason
[13374] Trade-offs
[13375] Security Impact
[13376] Performance Impact
[13377] Cost Impact
[13378] Operational Impact
[13379] Migration Impact
[13380] Date
[13381] Owner
[13383] 10.77 Technology Governance
[13384] Before introducing a new technology, assess:
[13385] business need
[13386] security
[13387] operational complexity
[13388] licensing
[13389] scalability
[13390] reliability
[13391] supportability
[13392] team expertise
[13393] cost
[13394] vendor lock-in
[13395] migration difficulty
[13396] The platform should avoid unnecessary technology proliferation.
[13398] 10.78 Technical Ownership Model
[13399] Every major component should have an owner.
[13400] Example:
[13401] No critical component should have ambiguous ownership.
[13403] 10.79 Architecture Dependency Rule
[13404] Higher-risk business domains should minimize unnecessary dependencies.
[13405] For example:
[13406] Eligibility
[13407] ↓
[13408] Provider
[13409] ↓
[13410] Plan
[13411] should not require an unrelated notification service to succeed before returning a critical eligibility decision.
[13412] Non-critical downstream work should be asynchronous.
[13414] 10.80 Five Invisible Pillars — Technical Implementation
[13415] Performance

[13417] caching

[13420] asynchronous processing
[13421] connection pooling
[13422] pagination
[13423] optimized APIs
[13424] CDN
[13425] queue-based workloads
[13426] Security

[13428] zero trust

[13433] encryption
[13434] WAF
[13435] secrets management
[13436] audit
[13437] security monitoring
[13438] tenant isolation
[13439] Scalability
[13440] Implemented through:
[13441] stateless services
[13442] horizontal scaling
[13443] queue workers
[13444] distributed cache
[13445] partitioning
[13446] read replicas
[13447] modular architecture
[13448] Reliability
[13449] Implemented through:
[13450] retries
[13451] idempotency
[13452] circuit breakers
[13453] backups
[13454] failover
[13455] dead-letter queues
[13456] observability
[13457] disaster recovery
[13458] Usability
[13459] Implemented through:
[13460] consistent APIs
[13461] role-aware interfaces
[13462] meaningful error messages
[13463] responsive applications
[13464] accessible interfaces
[13465] clear workflows
[13466] status tracking
[13467] progress indicators
[13468] understandable notifications
[13470] 10.81 Critical Architecture Principle
[13471] The architecture must never confuse:
[13472] System availability
[13473] with:
[13474] Permission to perform an operation.
[13475] A system being operational does not mean every authenticated user can perform every action.
[13476] Likewise:
[13477] A technical administrator must not automatically become a business approver.
[13478] This distinction is fundamental to the HMO platform.

[13481] Consider a claims officer approving a claim.
[13482] Claims Officer
[13483] ↓
[13484] Login

[13486] MFA

[13488] Session Created
[13489] ↓

[13528] STOP

[13538] Authentication

[13541] +

[13547] +

[13553] must be evaluated where applicable.
